How to Treat Age Spots Without Damaging Your Skin
2026-09-10

How to Treat Age Spots Without Damaging Your Skin

TIME | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings If your face, arms, or hands have started accumulating age spots, you may be wondering whether you can safely fade—or completely remove—them. The answer is probably yes. But before you start trying to erase anything, see a dermatologist. What people casually call an “age spot” could be one of several different skin changes, some of which require medical attention rather than a brightening serum. “The most important point is not to treat the words ‘age spot’ as a diagnosis,” says Dr. Erum Ilyas, a board-certified dermatologist in King of Prussia, Pa. “Before focusing on how to fade or remove a spot, first make sure you know what you’re treating.” Here’s how dermatologists recommend identifying age spots, treating them, and preventing more from appearing. A true age spot, also known as a solar lentigo, is a harmless patch of excess pigment often caused by cumulative exposure. It’s usually flat, evenly tan or brown, and has a smooth, well-defined border, says Dr. Rebecca Dufner, a board-certified dermatologist in Plano, Texas. It also tends to “sit there quietly year after year.” But people often use “age spot” to describe all sorts of marks that become more common over time. These can include seborrheic keratoses, which are benign growths that may look raised, waxy, or “stuck on,” as well as actinic keratoses, precancerous growths that are often rough or scaly. More concerningly, certain skin cancers—including melanoma—can resemble an ordinary brown spot. That’s why it’s important to have a dermatologist examine your spots before cosmetically treating them, particularly if you’ve never had them checked. Pay close attention to a spot that appears suddenly; changes in size, shape, or color; becomes raised; or starts itching, bleeding, crusting, or refusing to heal. Dufner also recommends using the “ugly duckling” rule: If one spot simply looks different from all of its neighbors, it deserves professional attention. “If your eye keeps being drawn to a spot because something about it seems different or unfamiliar, it’s worth having it examined,” Ilyas says. Dermatologists can inspect it with a dermatoscope, which reveals patterns and features that aren’t visible to the naked eye. If necessary, they can biopsy it. Once a dermatologist has confirmed you’re dealing with harmless age spots, you can try fading them at home. Just prepare to be patient: Topical treatments are “a slow game—think months, not days,” Dufner says. Prescription retinoids and azelaic acid are among the strongest topical options, she says. Over-the-counter products containing vitamin C, niacinamide, kojic acid, arbutin, glycolic acid, tranexamic acid, or thiamidol may also gradually lighten the spots. Hydroquinone can be effective, but it’s best used under a dermatologist’s supervision; prolonged overuse can cause a difficult-to-treat blue-black discoloration called exogenous ochronosis. Start with one active ingredient rather than assembling an elaborate—and potentially irritating—skin-care chemistry experiment. Because older skin tends to be thinner, drier, and more fragile, Dufner recommends beginning slowly, moisturizing regularly, and scaling back if your skin becomes irritated. Your kitchen isn’t a dermatology clinic. Dufner advises skipping lemon juice, apple-cider vinegar, and hydrogen peroxide, which “can irritate or even burn the skin” and potentially leave behind more discoloration. Baking soda and aggressive at-home peels can also damage the skin and trigger post-inflammatory hyperpigmentation. Don’t pick, scrape, burn, or attempt to freeze a growth at home, either. These approaches can cause “chemical burns, infection, scarring, or permanent pigment changes,” Murphy-Rose says. Plus, you could inadvertently remove a lesion that should have been biopsied. Dufner also recommends avoiding high-strength, unregulated “skin-bleaching” creams sold online. If a product promises to erase years of sun damage almost instantly, consider that a reason to be suspicious. In-office procedures generally work faster than topical products. For true age spots, “the heavy hitters are lasers and light-based treatments,” Dufner says. These procedures target excess pigment and can clear spots more quickly and completely than creams. Depending on your skin and the extent of the discoloration, a dermatologist might use a laser to treat a few defined spots or a light-based treatment to address more widespread, freckle-like sun damage. Other options include cryotherapy, which freezes individual spots with liquid nitrogen, and chemical peels. Seborrheic keratoses require a different approach because they’re “benign skin growths rather than simply excess pigment,” Murphy-Rose says. Once a dermatologist has confirmed the diagnosis, they can simply be left alone. If a growth becomes irritated, catches on clothing, or bothers you cosmetically, a dermatologist might remove it using cryotherapy or a shave procedure. The best method depends on the spot’s thickness, size, and location, as well as the patient’s skin type. None of these procedures is automatically the best choice for everyone. An overly aggressive treatment—or a laser used at the wrong setting—can cause irritation, scarring, or unwanted dark or light patches. Skin tone is especially important. People with more melanin-rich skin have a greater risk of developing post-inflammatory hyperpigmentation or hypopigmentation after certain procedures, particularly cryotherapy, Murphy-Rose says. Look for a board-certified dermatologist who has experience performing the proposed treatment on people with your skin tone. Age matters, too. Mature skin may bruise or tear more easily and take longer to heal. “Gentler is smarter,” Dufner says. A dermatologist might use lower laser settings, introduce topical ingredients more gradually, or allow additional healing time between treatments. Even after successful treatment, age spots can return—and new ones may continue appearing. That’s partly because “many of the spots we see today reflect sun exposure that occurred years or even decades ago,” Ilyas says. “We can’t undo that past exposure,” so additional spots may become visible even after existing ones have been treated. Consistent sun protection can, however, reduce additional UV damage and help keep existing pigmentation from becoming darker. Murphy-Rose recommends applying a broad-spectrum sunscreen with an SPF of at least 30 every day, as well as wearing hats and sun-protective clothing. Even after an age spot has been removed, she says, new ones “can develop if the skin continues to receive significant UV exposure.” Sunscreen also gives topical treatments a better chance to work. Without it, Dufner says, “you’re trying to fade the spots while UV exposure is telling the skin to make more pigment at the same time.” Her advice is simple: “Treat the spot, then protect your skin from the sun so you don’t keep making new ones.”

How Two Citizen Sleuths Spent Decades Trying to Solve the Double Homicide of A Killer Story
2026-09-10

How Two Citizen Sleuths Spent Decades Trying to Solve the Double Homicide of A Killer Story

TIME | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The tip came in 2017 from Alma, Kansas, from a stay-at-home mother. Eileen Umbehr asked whether Joe Sexton, a veteran investigative journalist who worked at The New York Times and ProPublica, wanted to know the truth about a double homicide in Topeka. Karen Harkness, daughter of a bigtime Topeka businessman, and her boyfriend Mike Sisco, a divorced father of two kids, were murdered on July 7, 2002, in the middle of the night, and authorities were convinced that Sisco’s ex-wife Dana Chandler was the killer. She was arrested, tried, and convicted of first-degree murder. Sexton, who thought the case was closed, emailed Umbehr back a polite “thanks, but no thanks.” Umbehr kept writing Sexton, anyway. Some weeks brought 10 emails, then none for two, then 25 in a single day, packed with packed with court records, ballistics reports, property filings. Her correspondence grew from hundreds to thousands of letters. Sexton wasn’t sure whether to call the police on Eileen or hire her for her thoroughness. But he gave her a nickname: “pen pal.” By then, Umbehr and her husband Keen, a lawyer, had spent years working the case and advocating for Chandler at their own expense. Then came email 2,714. The Kansas Supreme Court, Umbehr explained, had thrown out Chandler’s murder convictions, a rare occurrence. Sexton replied in six words: “Perhaps I should come see you.” Those six words began a journey which is now the subject of A Killer Story, a three-part documentary premiering Sept. 10 on HBO. Featuring Sexton alongside the married true-crime enthusiasts who recruited him—strangers to Chandler when they took up her case—it unspools the story of the deaths of Sisco and Harkness, and the case against Chandler, who was convicted in 2012, granted a new trial in 2018, retried to a hung jury in 2022, and convicted again in 2025. Directed by Matthew Galkin and executive produced by Joshua Levine, the series, filmed across the final six years of the case, explains how the final verdict was reached, without pretending a verdict also doubles as a definitive answer. Its deeper subject is the more amorphous notion of certainty: how detectives, prosecutors, and true-crime believers cling to it, and the collateral damage it can leave behind. The initial draw to the project, according to Levine, was how much material piled up before their cameras even arrived to capture it. “There were two decades, really, before we even came into it, where there were all these other layers beyond the crime itself,” he tells TIME. Those layers built up at the intersection of the media and a couple whose devotion, and what it cost them, kept changing the course of the case. Journalist Joe Sexton and director Matthew Galkin Courtesy of HBO The facts of what took place on July 7, 2002 are clear. Harkness and Sisco spent the previous evening at a casino, cameras catching them until they left around 1:30 a.m. They were home at her townhouse before 3. Harkness’ father later found their bodies; both had been shot repeatedly. Investigators ruled out robbery almost immediately. Cash lay in plain view and in Sisco’s wallet; Harkness still wore her jewelry. There were no fingerprints on the shell casings, no eyewitnesses, and no gun. The ammunition, though, was distinctive: Israeli-made subsonic rounds most commonly fired from an Uzi-style weapon. To Ray Lundin, then of the Kansas Bureau of Investigation, the scene read as “overkill,” a murder driven by emotion, as he puts it in the docuseries. Sisco’s family immediately suspected his ex-wife. Two people nearly 40 miles west, watching the same case unfold, would eventually conclude otherwise. Chandler made out poorly following a bitter divorce after 15 years of marriage, which had been strained by her drinking; Sisco kept the house and custody of the children, and received a child-support order in his favor. She went on to earn a master’s degree in accounting, got sober, and moved to Denver—540 miles and roughly eight hours from Topeka. When Detective Richard Volle interviewed her days after the killings, she walked him through her errands on July 6, including a stop at an AutoZone. What she did not mention was on the receipts: two five-gallon gas cans. Investigators built a theory around them. With enough fuel aboard, a driver could make it from Denver to Topeka and back without stopping. For nearly a decade, this remained only a theory. District Attorney Robert Hecht declined to charge Chandler, noting, as he does in the docuseries, that no one placed her in Topeka around the time of the murders. But his successors saw the case differently. Authorities arrested Chandler in Oklahoma in 2011, and in March 2012 a Topeka jury only needed 83 minutes to convict her of two counts of first-degree murder. She was sentenced to consecutive life terms with no possibility of parole for 100 years. At the trial, prosecutor Jacqie Spradling had told jurors that Sisco obtained a protection from abuse (PFA) order against Chandler, and Volle testified to it. In Kansas, a court issues a PFA order to protect someone from abuse by a household member or intimate partner. When Keen Umbehr, Eileen’s husband and by then Chandler’s research attorney, went hunting for the PFA, he could find none. The prosecution’s decision to tell the jury that one existed, he says in the docuseries, was a way of suggesting that Chandler was dangerous. On appeal, the Kansas Supreme Court found that no such order existed. In April 2018, the justices reversed both convictions, but also found the circumstantial evidence legally sufficient, which is why the reversal led to a retrial rather than Chandler’s release. Spradling's reckoning arrived on a separate track. Keen filed an ethics complaint against her in 2016; a disciplinary panel recommended disbarment five years later, and in May 2022 the Kansas Supreme Court disbarred her. Over the two decades that followed, the Umbehrs gave the case nearly everything they had. had given the case most of two decades, which raises the question Galkin partly built the series around: Why are these people so obsessed with this case? “They will dedicate their lives, give up their financial security, to defend someone who, at the beginning of the process, they did not know personally at all,” Galkin tells TIME. The couple is, on paper, an improbable pair of crusaders. High school sweethearts, they settled in Alma, where Keen hauled trash for a living until 1989 while writing a muckraking column for a local newspaper. When Wabaunsee County commissioners terminated his contract, in what he contended was retaliation for the columns, he sued, and in 1996 the U.S. Supreme Court ruled that independent contractors are protected from government retaliation for their speech. He eventually went to law school and opened a practice. Eileen, a lifelong true-crime devotee, says in the docuseries that her own drive came from faith and scripture’s instruction to speak for those who cannot. After Chandler’s 2012 conviction, Eileen wrote to her until she agreed to meet; Keen became her research attorney. Eventually he closed his practice, and the couple sold 500 acres of family land to work the case full time. Both were convinced of Chandler’s innocence. Levine, asked how two strangers became her most devoted advocates, tells the origin story in a different order. “They quickly realized that the son-in-law of Karen Harkness, one of the victims, was someone that they knew and had encountered over the years,” he says. They believed he could have done it, and that no one was watching him. “I think that was their entry point into it.” The suspicion of a man they knew came first, in 2002, nine years before the state charged Chandler. The certainty of her innocence followed. Galkin does not pretend the sequence is incidental. “It’s not just that they are convinced that Dana Chandler is not guilty; it’s that they are also convinced that they know who did it,” he says. The Umbehrs in A Killer Story Courtesy of HBO The man they suspected was Jeff Sutton, husband of Harkness’ daughter Erin, and formerly the county attorney of rural Wabaunsee County. The Umbehrs compiled a 200-page dossier on Sutton, asserting a history of violence, access to the townhouse, a federal license to deal in high-powered weapons, and an unusual arrangement that let him take confiscated guns from the county sheriff’s inventory as payment for casework. Keen came to believe one gun missing from that inventory was an Uzi, and a sheriff’s deputy told him she had personally seen Sutton with that type of weapon. The couple sent the dossier to virtually every law enforcement agency in Kansas, though none responded. What they wanted was a comparison. The lone piece of DNA in the case is a single hair singed onto a shell casing found in the bedroom, which prosecutors tested only on the eve of the 2012 trial.The results matched neither victim, nor Chandler. Keen developed a theory based on the test results: casings eject to the right, so a left-handed shooter’s right forearm hangs above the arc, where a hot casing could sear off a hair on its way down. For the theory to work, Sutton had to be left-handed. Eileen found a newspaper photograph of him signing his oath of office as a prosecutor, which showed that he was. Using a Kansas provision that lets any citizen submit what amounts to a probable-cause affidavit, the Umbehrs went before a judge to compel his DNA. Sutton, remarkably, sat for the docuseries, after what Levine describes as years of careful groundwork by Sexton. “We didn’t know what Jeff Sutton was going to say in that interview chair,” Galkin says. But what he says is unequivocal: He denies killing Sisco and Harkness, and says Keen held a grudge against him but he “never had a beef” with Keen, despite the fact that Sutton was a target of some of Keen’s early muckraking, dating back to the late 1980s. No law enforcement agency ever named him a suspect. When the DNA testing results came back, the hair on the shell casing proved not to be his. Still, the Umbehrs’ suspicions around Sutton persisted. “People believe what they want to believe, rightly or wrongly,” Levine says. “It blinds you from the truth sometimes.” Galkin and Levine had no way of knowing how, or even whether, any of these open questions would resolve. Production began before Chandler’s second trial, meaning the filmmakers committed to covering a murder story with no ending in sight. The resolution came in installments, the first in the summer of 2022, when the second trial opened and prosecutor Charles Kitt conceded at the outset the ground the defense had held for 20 years. “This case is not based on DNA,” he told jurors. “This case is not based on hair. This case is not based on fingerprints. This case is based on jealousy, rage, and obsession.” Defense attorney Tom Bath called it a textbook argument for the presumption of innocence. On the stand, Volle conceded what the Supreme Court had already established: there was never a PFA order. Chandler’s own conflicting accounts of what she did and where she was the day before and the day of the killings did the most damage to her case. A friend, Jeff Bailey, testified that weeks after her police interview, she gave him a different story entirely: She had been camping alone in the mountains, sleeping in her car, and had given police a different alibi because she doubted they would accept the true one. Then, on day 11, prosecutors announced a surprise witness. Terri Anderson, a neighbor of Harkness’, testified that late that night she heard gunshots, saw movement, and briefly saw a face she believed was Chandler’s, and that she called 911. But a records custodian could produce no record of the call, or of any police response. Casino cameras showed Sisco and Harkness alive well past the hour she described. The jury deliberated six days and split—seven for conviction, five against—resulting in a mistrial. A week after her testimony, Anderson sat down with Sexton, who walked her through the records on camera. “I’m second-guessing myself right now, and I’m thinking, could I have misremembered something?” she says in the docuseries. “I wish the prosecutors would have shown me this prior to me testifying.” If they had, she would have asked to be excused. “I would not put myself out there if I was going to tell a blatant lie about anything.” Levine is gentle in his opinion of Anderson. “I don’t blame her,” he says. “I think she truly believed what she testified to.” Prosecutors elected to try Chandler a third time, and the case moved to the small town of Westmoreland, far from the Topeka jury pool. With opening statements about to begin in February 2025, Chandler dismissed her defense team to represent herself. She had never testified in the previous trials, but now she would conduct her entire defense, including the cross-examination of her own children, who would be witnesses for the state as they had been in past trials. “How rare to see, in the third trial, a mother cross-examine her daughter,” Levine says. “It’s just unbelievable. For the family, they just have to keep reliving this publicly.” On the stand for days, Chandler told her life story: born in New Mexico before moving to Colorado where she met Sisco, had children, began drinking, and endured a marriage she called “so broken and toxic” that sobriety kept slipping away. During cross examination, the prosecution laid her different alibis side by side: the errands-and-early-bedtime she gave Volle, the solo camping she gave Bailey, the version she now offered the jury. In closing, Kitt told jurors the case was not finally about revenge or jealousy but control. Chandler, during her own closing remarks, reminded the jury there was no evidence she was in Topeka at the time of the killings or that she ever purchased or owned a 9-mm firearm. “I submit to you that the state has woefully failed to meet its burden to prove beyond a reasonable doubt that I killed Mike Sisco or that I killed Karen Harkness,” she said. The jury needed hours, not days, and they found Chandler guilty on both counts. Galkin suggests Chandler’s decision to represent herself this time was the wrong one. “You just sort of watch someone squander a really good opportunity to not be in prison for the rest of her life,” he says. The sentence this time: consecutive life terms with no parole eligibility for 50 years. Ask the filmmakers for their own verdict, and what comes back is uncertainty. “I think Josh and I are still, seven years on, in this mindset of: We don’t really know if [Dana] did it or not,” Galkin says. The absence of physical evidence keeps him circling. “Just when you think that you have convinced yourself that she did do it ... you start questioning: Well, how did she do it?” he adds. “Because she would have had to pull off the perfect murder.” For years, one voice was missing from the chorus: Chandler’s. Only when she fired her lawyers to represent herself, then testified, did the filmmakers hear her perspective at length; the one time she insisted on telling it entirely herself, with her freedom in the balance, a jury rejected it. Sexton’s observation from the courtroom, as Galkin recalls it, was that “knowing your story and arguing it well are two very different things.” Galkin puts his own opinion more directly: “Regardless of whether she told her own story compellingly or not, she did not argue her case well.” Levine wants viewers to finish the docuseries asking the same questions he and Galkin have not stopped asking themselves. Galkin’s wish, meanwhile, runs even further against the grain of the true crime genre: for the audience to manage what almost no one in this story could, by sitting with a case that’s “beautifully unsatisfying.” It is an unusual wish, but an honest one. More than two decades of presumed certainty produced three trials and a family broken in ways no verdict could ever repair. The genre conditions its viewers to expect a clear-cut answer, but A Killer Story asks them to live without one.

9/11 and the Substitute Mother
2026-09-10

9/11 and the Substitute Mother

TIME | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Diana Miller and Daphne "Saza" Pouletsos. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings In 2001, I lost the world I knew. Twenty-five years later, I’m accidentally rebuilding it. “I think people think you’re my mother,” my six-year-old niece whispered to me as we walked through the parking lot toward CVS. “I think you’re right,” I said, smiling back at her impish grin that suggested we were pulling one over on the world. I had picked her up from school and was indulging her request to buy fake nails for our sleepover. She gripped my hand tightly, as a daughter would. We proudly walked through the automatic sliding doors, as one. I don’t have children of my own, which means my niece is the beneficiary of my pent-up maternal love and obsession. She was born in 2020, and four months later, I left my job as the executive producer of a morning news show in New York and moved to Los Angeles. It was the first time I’d disrupted my ambitious climb up the media ladder, and when my feet landed in the California sand, my role as an aunt became the foundation of my daily life. Birthdays, ballet recitals, and school performances anchored my calendar. Playing hide-and-seek before dinner, getting ice cream on a Tuesday, and letting her empty my closet for dress-up replaced late-night calls, breaking news, and media dinners. Recently, when my sister traveled abroad for work, my niece wanted assurance that her “substitute mother” would be around. The phrase made me laugh, but also squeezed my heart like a vise. Because I know what it’s like to have a substitute mother. Daphne Pouletsos worked for the insurance company AON, her office was located in the south tower of the World Trade Center, and she was among the 2,977 victims killed in the 9/11 attacks. Saza, as we called her, was my mother’s younger sister. Her nickname was a name given by my oldest cousin, Dayna, whose toddler tongue couldn’t say “Aunt Daphne.” Although Saza lived in New Jersey and I grew up in Virginia, she was a constant light in my life. We drove up north for summers and holidays, and Saza would visit us throughout the year. Once, she hid in the back of our Chrysler minivan when my mom picked my siblings and me up from school, and she revealed herself by suddenly piping into the conversation. We squealed with excitement. Saza knew how to make a mundane moment sparkle. Her sense of humor was the first thing to enter the room, and sleepovers at her house meant staying up late to watch Saturday Night Live. In my young mind, “The Church Lady” and “Coffee Talk” were extensions of my aunt (it helped that she also had a New Jersey accent). Shimmying our shoulders like “Sprockets” was like a special inside joke. Her laugh could sweeten the sourest mood. But the best moments with her were always Christmas morning. Saza would come to my grandparents’ house first thing in the morning, the jingle bell on the back door announcing her arrival. She’d sit in the big, blue upholstered wingback chair, her feet tucked under her like a teenager, eyes shining behind her ‘90s rimless glasses. I can’t separate the thrill I got from opening a new baby doll from the joy I got from showing it to her. She would coo with enthusiasm, amplifying my delight. One time, she gave all of her nieces and nephews thick black plastic glasses with rubber banana noses attached. It was the silliest gift I’ve ever received, and one of the best. We took a picture, all piled on top of each other: laughing, goofy, family. Her presence carried a promise that everything was okay. As a kid, I assumed it always would be. Daphne Pouletsos' nieces nieces and nephews. Courtesy of Diana Miller. I was studying abroad in the fall of 2001. Classes at University College London hadn’t started yet, so I was traipsing around Europe with a sorority sister. We were at a restaurant in Barcelona when a waiter expressed dismay about “what happened at the World Trade Center.” “My aunt works there,” I told my friend. But I was sure she was fine. How could she not be? Still, I found a payphone and called home. My father tried to convey a stoic message: “We don’t know anything yet, we’re calling all the hospitals.” Then I saw the footage on the TV in our hostel. I couldn’t believe that she could be one of the victims. Not Saza. As a journalist, I know the questions that are typical in the wake of a tragedy. How old were they? Did she or he have kids? It’s natural. We’re inclined to measure loss by what, or rather, who, is left behind. But the true impact of a person’s life is not reflected in titles or preconceived roles. Every one of the 2,977 victims on 9/11 had a world that was wide and meaningful. You may not know the details of that world, but someone does. And they mourn not just the loss of that person, but the moments they made possible. For me, saying “I lost my aunt on September 11th” doesn’t begin to tell the story. My loss isn’t constricted by norms. Her legacy doesn’t fit neatly into a headline. How old was she? Forty-seven. No, she didn’t have kids, but she had us. Twenty-five years later, I am 45, and I have my niece. Saza was the person who gave me the gift of knowing what an aunt can be. While I could never fill her slippers, I feel Saza with me every day. She’s there when my niece and I are feeding the stray cat in my backyard. She’s there when we’re laughing at the Sunday comics. She’s there as we get dolled up in fancy dresses to go to the neighborhood pizza place. I am alive in these moments because she lived. I wonder what else her life would have included if she had kept living it. What it would be like to have her visit me in Los Angeles. How she, my sister, and I would stay up late watching SNL together. How much love she would pour into life’s middle moments. And most of all, what joy she would take in getting to know my niece, her namesake: Daphne. TIME Ideas hosts the world's leading voices, providing commentary on events in news, society, and culture. We welcome outside contributions. Opinions expressed do not necessarily reflect the views of TIME editors.

Trump’s Attacks on the Environment Are Out of Touch With America
2026-09-10

Trump’s Attacks on the Environment Are Out of Touch With America

TIME | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Follow this section to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this tag to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings President Donald Trump on the Ellipse of the White House on Sept. 8, 2026. Follow this author to personalize your feed and get instant alerts. Update your preferences in Account Settings The most pro-American stance a leader can take is to defend our best ideas. America's national parks and our nation’s beautiful environment are part of our heritage, identity, and are ultimately our greatest strength. From President Theodore Roosevelt dramatically expanding public lands protections to President Richard Nixon signing the Clean Air Act, our best days have come when protecting the environment was led by leaders on both sides of the aisle. The current administration is not honoring that shared American value. Nearly two years into President Donald Trump’s second term, his administration is failing the American people on the environment, on our national parks, and on the public lands that are supposed to unite us. Plenty of people in the environmental movement would’ve opposed anything pro-environment this president did on mere partisan principle. I’m not one of them. Early last year I worked closely with the president’s team and several cabinet officials to help shape a well-intentioned and potentially powerful executive order: “Make America Beautiful Again.” This initiative called on his cabinet to secure a historic investment in our public lands, our forests, our water, and outdoor recreation. I firmly believed in the concept, and I still believe in its potential, but we’ve gotten the opposite of what was promised. This president labels his policies “America first,” but on public lands and national parks he’s simply put America last. As someone who was in those rooms making the case, I could not be more disappointed. And it doesn’t need to be this way. Their record is dark. Thus far, the Trump Administration has gutted national park staff, opposed by around 86% of Americans. They’ve proposed sell-offs of public land, which is opposed by nearly 90% of voters in Western states, where these spaces are concentrated. They’ve prioritized rollbacks to policies like the Endangered Species Act, which is opposed by 85% of Americans. They’ve stripped protections for clean water, clean air, national forests, and more, also opposed by the vast majority of Americans. On issue after issue, this administration has landed on the wrong side of the American people on the environment. Public blowback has stopped a few of these decisions, but a new threat to the great American outdoors seems to surface almost every week. These are quite possibly the two worst years for our environment under a president in my lifetime. I’ve sat across from dozens of White House officials, and I can tell you there are people who care deeply about conservation. My sense is that their voices are being drowned out by those who primarily act in opposition to the Democratic Party, many of whom have never spent real time in America’s grandeur and do not understand what it means to the rest of us. They are doing the president a disservice. His single most popular act in his first term was arguably signing the immensely bipartisan Great American Outdoors Act, the largest investment in our national parks in history. Now, on an issue that could unify the country and win him support from every walk of life, he and his team are deliberately walking the other way. The President and his team could defend any one of these choices on its own, justified by claiming they’re making the government more efficient, that local management of our land is better, or that they are unleashing economic growth. Yet, taken together, their actions add up to a plain refusal to treat our environment and our public lands as a priority in any real sense. More than that, I am concerned by their lack of proactive, positive environmental policies. It’s backwards, and it cuts against the American voter and against our national character. Two things can exist simultaneously. The economy is, understandably, the country’s top concern right now. But it does not have to come at the cost of shrinking or dismantling the country’s natural resources and beauty. Smart, limited government does not require slashing America's grandest idea. It does not mean choosing polluters over citizens. It does not mean railroading projects through communities that never asked for them. It does not mean overriding the will of the people. Nearly a third of all land in the United States is publicly owned and managed by the federal government. The current approach compounds decades of these lands being mismanaged and desperately underfunded. Our air quality and water quality are worsening, invasive species are spreading at alarming rates, soil health is degrading, and much more. How we care for these resources should not be a partisan policy fight. It should be a productive dialogue that shapes the American landscape itself and improves the lives of families in every corner of the country. Our national parks aren’t political; they’re American. I am often told by friends on the political left that Trump will never make conservation a priority, that Republicans simply hate the environment. I understand the cynicism. But it is neither productive nor true to who Americans actually are. The vast majority of the American voters want the same things when it comes to our National Parks and environment. Our leaders, specifically around this Administration, just aren’t reflecting that right now. Outside of Washington, Americans would love to see this president, and every president, protect these places. My prediction: The Republican Party will continue to lose voters if they fail to protect our environment, and it should. Americans believe our nation’s beauty is worth defending. We ought to follow the public, not an endless political game of ping-pong. Americans don’t wear a party patch on their sleeve when it comes to the health of our environment. They wear pride in this nation's beauty. Today, a record-low share of Americans believe our environment is healthy. If there was ever a wrong moment to make cuts, this is it. Not when energy demand, AI, data centers, and screen addiction are straining this country like never before. Not when visits to National Parks and public lands are near an all-time high — and those lands need more care, not less. Not when Americans expect their tax dollars to build historic investment in our environment, not strip it away. America has always found a way, albeit imperfectly, to balance protecting our environment with growing our economy. We do not have to ruin our public lands to save our economy. Investing in the places that keep us whole benefits our mental health, our physical health, and our economic health. History will never look kindly on a leader who lets America’s most beloved places decline. President Trump has two years left to prove he’s better than that. He can work across partisan boundaries and accomplish popular, common-sense environmental wins, or he can be remembered as the worst president for America’s environment in modern history. The choice is his. And it shouldn’t be a hard one. TIME Ideas hosts the world's leading voices, providing commentary on events in news, society, and culture. We welcome outside contributions. Opinions expressed do not necessarily reflect the views of TIME editors.
The Atlantic
Jackie Oh!
2026-09-10

Jackie Oh!

The Atlantic | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Once, when I was 15 years old and as awkward and skinny-legged as a baby giraffe, I was idling in a department store in central London when I saw the novelist Jackie Collins standing at the register. Can you imagine the aura emanating from her, like Hermès-scented magnetic waves? She was wearing animal print and a tailored jacket and had the kind of steely composure I’ve never managed to pull off in adulthood without straining something. Faced with one of my true idols, all I could do was gawk. Check out more from this issue and find your next story to read. I learned about sex when I was 8, but it wasn’t until several years later, when my best friend yanked me upstairs to show me a particularly juicy paragraph from her mother’s neon-pink copy of Hollywood Kids, that I learned about pleasure. Jackie—with apologies, I will call her Jackie throughout, because “Collins” feels just too formal for a woman who impressed upon me the existence of the clitoris—once told The New York Times that she published her first novel “in 1968, when no one was writing about sex but Philip Roth.” Which wasn’t strictly true, but at a moment when second-wave feminism and sexual liberation were uneasy bedfellows, Jackie simply wrote her own revolution. The World Is Full of Married Men, her debut, was a caustic social satire in which a wronged wife emerges triumphant; The Stud, out the following year, was a flashy potboiler about a trophy wife and nightclub owner with a rapacious sexual appetite who treats men like toys and exalts in her social and financial clout. Jackie wrote 32 books in total, sold more than half a billion copies, and gained an indelible reputation as an airport novelist, the kind of writer whose books are equally devoured and disdained. But oh, what they did for me! In retrospect, I gained a slightly deluded understanding of what adulthood actually entailed. Jackie was a devoted Mickey Spillane fan, and her books tend to be stuffed with gangsters, serial killers, pornographers, and even freelance journalists who get paid in advance. But as an anatomist of Hollywood gender dynamics, no writer was sharper or more aspirational. Jackie’s heroines, as she wrote in an introduction to The World Is Full of Divorced Women (1975), “demand equality—sexual and otherwise.” She wrote about creative, ferocious women striving for power and getting it. Lucky Santangelo, a recurring character and a kind of alter ego, starts out as the neglected daughter of a crime boss and works her way up to become the head of a movie studio in 1990’s Lady Boss. Her characters carry on being fascinating and sexually active well past the age when other writers would have them tucked in bed with a crocheted blanket and a crossword puzzle. “In Jackie’s world, women’s real lives begin after forty,” the writer Helen Ellis observed of Hollywood Wives, the book that Jackie published at the age of 45, and that established her as a celebrity in her own right. Even if you hate women (many people do), hate explicit sex scenes, hate the kind of pulpy storytelling that has you still reading in a cold bath at midnight, you cannot help but admit that Jackie captured the entertainment industry like no one else. She seemed equally drawn to the business of movie production—the seedy dealmaking and kickbacks—as well as the rampant abuses of power embedded in the Hollywood system, both of which she exposed without hesitation. Writing in the heady 1980s, when movies were becoming brasher, dumber, and unrepentantly commercial, she scrutinized a creative industry at a notable low point, less in thrall to art than to profit. Although her novels made her a star, Jackie never considered herself fully part of Hollywood. Like a Reagan-era Margaret Mead, she took an anthropological approach. “I don’t live the Hollywood lifestyle,” she told Vanity Fair in 1995. “I move in it. I’m an observer.” At parties, she would stalk the room listening for interesting tidbits, taking copious mental notes that she’d jot down later. Describing an A-list event in Hollywood Wives, Jackie drops in fragments of what one character calls “Hollywood conversation,” dialogue that evokes a world where money and desire meld: “ ‘He pays me, I think it turns him on,’ said the redhead in the mink-trimmed cape.” So accurate was her portrayal that when Hollywood Wives was published, Jackie noted, “The ladies of Hollywood had their drivers and housekeepers lining up at the bookstores to get an early copy,” both furious that their gilded sanctum had been breached and desperate to pinpoint which character was based on which real person. Hollywood, as a geographical term at least, is misleading: Jackie’s characters mostly work and play in Beverly Hills, a city of 30,000 or so extremely wealthy residents enclosed by Los Angeles. Formerly a lima-bean farm, then a failed oil field, Beverly Hills was developed into lots for housing around the same time that the movie industry headed west, in the early 20th century. When the silent-film stars Mary Pickford and Douglas Fairbanks moved into a spectacular former hunting lodge on Summit Drive in 1920—the press immediately named it “Pickfair”—they helped establish Beverly Hills as a nexus of celebrity glamour and industry cachet. Jackie genuinely loved the neighborhood for its clean white lines and endless sky, but she was also drawn to everything it contained beneath the surface: its roiling currents of fame, power, and degradation. Early this summer, I traveled from London, where Jackie was born, to the enclave where she made her name. As a child of the ’80s, I was nostalgic for the maximalist era of shoulder pads, Giorgio perfume, and animal print. (Jackie loved big cats for their beauty and power, was convinced she’d been a panther in a previous life, and decorated her house and herself in leopard spots.) But as a cultural critic, I also wanted to revisit the era of moviemaking that Jackie had described in her novels. The ’80s saw a wave of corporate consolidation; venerable studios were gobbled up by behemoths from other industries. (Columbia Pictures was briefly a division of Coca-Cola.) This led to sillier, more crowd-pleasing movies. Even so, Hollywood was at the apex of its power, the global influence of filmmaking more potent than ever. Today, the center of cultural power and attention is shifting away from Los Angeles. Traditional storytelling has lost ground to what the consulting firm McKinsey calls “user-generated content”—TikTok and YouTube videos in which people talk enthusiastically about peptides and skinny-maxxing and the cruel feminization of the male life force. As a teenager, I had learned from Jackie’s books to expect that adulthood for women would be a series of hard-won triumphs that lead toward power. (She also suggested that midlife would involve a lot of stalking around restaurants while wearing crepe Halston jumpsuits and enough gold jewelry to sink a yacht, but I have long since made peace with neutral basics.) I am 43, roughly the age when Jackie’s heroines enter their prime, and when she herself did. I am very tired, having spent the past 14 months working, raising two 5-year-olds, promoting a book, and falling into existential mires of despair over the degenerating state of world affairs. A small, quiet part of my brain wondered whether tracing Jackie’s literary footsteps might reinvigorate my sense of what’s possible. Or at least demonstrate that her dreams for women—that they can have creative fulfillment, families, and happiness, as she did—might be somewhat more realistic now than they were 40 years ago. Hollywood Wives was published in 1983, a good two decades before reality television turned exposing the lives of the wealthy, married, and highly maintained into a Bravo gold mine. We’re accustomed to such excess now, but back then, Jackie’s gossipy revelations—$50 panties, discreet plastic surgeons in Palm Springs, society grandes dames who used to be French call girls—seemed otherworldly. The primary antiheroine in the book is the delightfully unlikable Elaine Conti (imagine a hornier, more desperate Becky Sharp), the 39-year-old wife of a movie star, Ross, whose last film bombed at the box office. There’s also a former gigolo on the brink of movie stardom, a buxom man-eater who wants to be taken seriously as an actress, and a serial killer who calls himself the “Keeper of the Order.” But for me at least, the book stands out for its portrayal of early-’80s Beverly Hills as a high-society Thunderdome, where everyone is scratching and clawing for status. In addition to the titular wives, who are drawn with both humanity and mild disdain, Jackie offers up a number of female characters operating in what she describes as the “Big Boys’ Club” of the movie industry: Sadie LaSalle, one of the most powerful and fearsome agents in town, and Montana Gray, a writer trying to save her screenplay from the clutches of a ruthless producer. This is a world where power-lunching demands tactical precision. Elaine’s mission throughout the book is to restore Ross’s A-list status, with buzzy parties the couple can’t afford and a standing Friday lunch reservation at Ma Maison, where “anybody who was anybody and in town invariably showed up.” Ma Maison closed in 1985, and so the first place I went to test out the current state of power-dining was the Polo Lounge, a clubby salon situated toward the back of the spectacularly retro Beverly Hills Hotel. In Hollywood Husbands, Jackie’s 1986 follow-up, the handsome and promiscuous talk-show host Jack Python regularly meets his best friends for breakfast there, where they occupy Table One, “a cosy leather booth directly facing the entrance.” Table placement, Jackie emphasizes, is everything—“The back of this restaurant is Siberia,” one model tells another late in the book. “Land of the underachievers. Don’t even glance in that direction.” I was not placed at Table One. I was seated just outside what seemed to be the main dining room, next to an elderly couple and a 12-year-old wearing a World Cup hoodie having pancakes by himself. Through the doorway, I could see men in button-downs and golf shirts conversing chummily; I started to feel slightly psychotic glimmers of envy and shame at being shut out from whatever they were brokering. The server’s eyes expressed gentle but unmistakable pity. A handsome Englishman was seated at a nearby table but immediately sensed defeat and asked to be moved to the terrace, where the sun shines regardless of a diner’s Variety profile, stock portfolio, or follower count. Failure is galvanizing, I tried to remember from Jackie’s books, as long as you don’t let it break you. For much of her early life, Jackie wasn’t known for her writing or her feline fixation. (A plaque engraved with a panther marks her place in the celebrity crypt at Westwood Village Memorial Park Cemetery, where she was interred after her death, in 2015; the image is accompanied by the words SHE GAVE A GREAT DEAL OF PEOPLE A GREAT DEAL OF PLEASURE.) She came of age as the taller, ungainly younger sister of Joan, an almond-eyed, preternaturally composed actress who made her Hollywood debut starring alongside Bette Davis in 1955’s The Virgin Queen. Joan and Jackie had grown up together in a basement apartment in dingy postwar London, the daughters of a dance teacher and a talent agent whose parties, Jackie once said, were where she first became aware of how men talk about women when they think no one’s listening. “I get an awful inferiority complex when I’m with Joan,” the teenage Jackie wrote in her diary. In a photo of the pair together from 1955, Joan stands front and center, insouciant in a fur coat, while Jackie looks at the camera semi-apologetically from behind her. Jackie was, however, entrepreneurial; by the age of 13, inspired by Harold Robbins and Henry Miller, she had a sideline writing sex scenes longhand and charging her classmates to read them. When Joan’s career took her to Hollywood, Jackie followed, moving with her sister to an apartment in the Chateau Marmont hotel, and having a brief fling—at 15—with the 29-year-old Marlon Brando. (He was “only my height, and kind of fat,” she noted in her journal, and also recorded that he called her “sincere, sweet, and luscious.”) But Jackie couldn’t get a work permit and so reluctantly returned to England, where she appeared in a handful of schlocky B movies before marrying the businessman Wallace Austin at 22. Four years later, Austin’s drug addiction had led the couple to divorce, and he died of an overdose shortly after. It was Jackie’s second husband, the producer and nightclub owner Oscar Lerman, who encouraged her to pursue writing, and to draw inspiration from the vivid social scenes the couple mixed in. (At one of her husband’s clubs, she told The Hollywood Reporter, Lerman had to put a guard outside the men’s room because teenage girls kept following movie stars in to offer them sexual favors.) At 31, she published The World Is Full of Married Men, which was an immediate best seller in the United Kingdom and promptly banned in Australia for its inventive array of four-letter words; the next year, the romance novelist Barbara Cartland lambasted her new novel, The Stud, as “awful, terrible, it’s evil really.” Jackie and Lerman relocated to Los Angeles in 1980, and if you walk down the Beverly Hills Hotel’s unabashedly red-carpeted stairs and under a gloriously cinematic green-and-white striped awning, then stroll to the bottom of the hill, and wander for about 10 minutes beneath towering palm trees, you will arrive at the house that Jackie’s “awful, terrible” books built. Only a modest-seeming driveway was visible from the street. The place is now available for rent (for the trivial sum of $175,000 a month), and when I say that I begged the real-estate agent to let me view it, please know that I really and truly groveled. I spent months emailing a man who assured me that he was entreating the owners (real-estate developers who’d bought it after her death, gutted it, remodeled all the interiors) on my behalf—but to no avail. So instead I examined the signs affixed to palm trees warning me that I was on camera, to ensure the security of the neighborhood’s current residents, who—unlike the movie stars and moguls of Jackie’s day—are apparently financiers, minor Middle Eastern royals, and crypto magnates. (The current owner of the Pickfair mansion, I later learned, is the technology corporation Unicom Global, which uses it for conferences and events.) But based on the listing, archival photographs, and the author photo on Jackie’s many hardcovers, I can tell you that it’s a 22,000-square-foot museum of a mansion, a defiant middle finger to the critics who belittled her for writing “bonkbusters,” and a visual testament to her success. The interiors are now predominantly beige, but in Jackie’s day, the pillows were all animal print. She designed the house around a huge rectangular swimming pool after seeing David Hockney’s A Bigger Splash in London. On her book jackets, she stands, radiant and shoulder-padded, next to the cool blue water and her imposing white box of a home. But she also took great pride in being a hands-on mother, cooking at home for her three daughters, doing school drop-offs in her silver Mustang, and keeping her children away from the sleazy Hollywood scenes she chronicled. Right as Jackie’s writing started appearing on the best-seller list, Joan’s acting career was floundering—in 1977, she appeared in the dismal sci-fi horror Empire of the Ants, playing a land developer set on by giant irradiated insects. It was Jackie who revived her sister’s reputation, after Joan was cast as the lead in the movie adaptation of The Stud. (A hit in Britain, the movie paved the way for Joan to get her most indelible American role, as Alexis Colby in Dynasty.) The charged sense of competition between the pair continued apace; in a 1988 Vanity Fair profile of both sisters, Dominick Dunne noted that a publicist had instructed him to dedicate equal copy to each woman, while also citing the rumors that Silver Anderson, the prickly 47-year-old TV star in Hollywood Husbands, was based on Joan. Mindful of the security cameras, I walked quickly past Jackie’s house to a restaurant in downtown Beverly Hills called Bacari, where I took out my notebook and tried my hand at Jackie-style anthropology. At the table next to me, a talent agent was trying to lure another agent away from a major conglomerate, where she was unhappy. “They keep trying to buy us off with ice-cream socials,” she said to him plaintively. “Everyone is on a diet!” The conversation turned to her stable of clients, who all seemed to be social-media stars. She spoke proudly about one woman she represented who, under her mentorship, went from having 700,000 followers to more than 1 million. “That’s the kind of nurturing I bring to my job,” she said. The man asked her if she might be able to double her client list in the first three months; to help her in the effort, he could offer her a “digital assistant—no person.” He paid the bill. “Let’s get those wheels turning,” he said, ushering her out, and I wondered what Jackie would make of this version of Hollywood, with its brand-activation opportunities and AI-drafted emails, where celebrity is its own art form and product is paramount. In Jackie’s Hollywood novels, Rodeo Drive—my next stop—is where the women of Beverly Hills go to arm themselves for the soft warfare of self-presentation. When every outfit, every hairdo, is minutely studied for signs of decline, keeping up appearances is essential. In Hollywood Husbands, Poppy Solomon, the secretary turned fourth wife of a studio head, gets ready for parties in much the same way General Patton commanded the Third Army: with ostentatious and pleasurable aggression. (“She wore a turquoise frilled, strapless dress, and many real diamonds. Her new tits protruded nicely.”) The day I was there, the picture-perfect palms gently fluttering down Rodeo bore signs for an upcoming car show, which seemed unnecessary given that every day brings a parade of tricked-out vehicles: I watched a procession of Porsches, G-Wagons, two separate Maybachs with hydraulic suspension systems that had them bouncing up and down like sugared-up toddlers. No one was actually shopping, despite the lavish window displays. Instead, everyone was posing for pictures, wearing outfits they’d put together for this exact purpose and drafting husbands and friends to play photographer. A slender blond woman clutching a tan Birkin pouted while reclining against a lamppost outside Stefano Ricci. Someone whispered that she was a Russian influencer with a superstar-athlete partner, which seemed perfectly plausible, but outside the subset of her followers, how would the rest of us know? I was accompanied by a photographer, Dina, who approached the influencer to take her picture, but the woman scuttled away like a cartoon mouse. Something odd was happening, we quickly realized: Everyone was out posing in public, but no one wanted to cede control of how they might be perceived. When Dina tried to take photos of the Beverly Hills Hotel from a public sidewalk, a voice instantly came over a loudspeaker warning her to stop. In a weak attempt to consider some of the box-office excess of 1980s Hollywood (Footloose, Top Gun, Beverly Hills Cop), I booked us a Paramount Pictures studio tour, but when I asked a publicist about Dina bringing her camera, I was told approval would be required, a studio representative would accompany us, and anything said on the tour could not be quoted. (All that’s worth saying about what followed is that while being driven around the lot where Citizen Kane, Breakfast at Tiffany’s, and Chinatown were shot, the most enthralling thing we saw was the plasticky interior set from the CBS reboot of Matlock.) Jackie would find Hollywood’s new power dynamics perplexing: Many of her female characters are writers and reporters on the movie-celebrity beat, who see their job as pushing past the sanitized narratives that publicists try to present. But as media jobs have dwindled, influencers have stepped in to fill a void. Why would you trust a possibly unfriendly reporter with access to an actor or a director or a TV set when you can invite a photogenic social-media star to a premiere and be assured that they’ll rave about whatever they see to their hundreds of thousands of followers? In Hollywood Husbands, Silver, the soap star possibly based on Joan, is interviewed by an English journalist, “a middle-aged woman with brittle looks and dyed yellow hair” who sucks up to her subjects as energetically in person as she tears them apart in print. “So what?” Silver thinks. “Someone had once said As long as they spell your name right.” The internet has long since retooled this bargain. And what are celebrities now, anyway? During my four days in Beverly Hills, which Jackie portrays as a superstar playground—Clint Eastwood, Jack Lemmon, Richard Gere, and Tom Selleck all feature in one lunch scene at Ma Maison—I saw not a single recognizably famous person. A dozen or so women looked similar enough to Kim Kardashian to stop me short; they were clearly modeled after her, but the image fell apart on second glance. One night I went to dinner at Funke, a purported newish hot spot for stars (Gwyneth Paltrow is a fan) where the chef used to have his own show on the disastrous short-form streaming platform Quibi. No sightings. My L.A.-based friends advised heading east, to Los Feliz and Silver Lake, or west, to Venice and Santa Monica. Beverly Hills today is a bastion of the über-wealthy—Jeff Bezos owns the estate built by Jack Warner of Warner Bros.—and a drop-by for people who come to pose for Instagram. Like SoHo in New York and Notting Hill in London, the place feels like a simulacrum of a film set, where everyone is perpetually performing for unseen audiences and nothing feels quite real. In her books, Jackie is unsentimental about creative work. Her characters tend to hustle for money and power, not self-indulgent awards or critical acclaim. When good movies get made, it’s largely by accident, because someone took their eye off the ball. But one character in Hollywood Wives seems to genuinely care about the integrity of the work she makes: Montana Gray, the tall, raven-haired, strikingly offbeat writer who—coincidentally—has a biography that seems a lot like Jackie’s own. By 15, Montana is a worldly soul who’s traveled the globe, learned French and Italian, and had two affairs. After spending a few years in bohemian circles, she meets the much older film director Neil Gray in Paris and falls in love with him. She helps him get sober and working again; he encourages her to write. At the start of the novel, her gritty screenplay, Street People, is the talk of Hollywood, with actors and agents fighting for copies. And, because Montana is the wife of a major director, no one can accept that she actually wrote it. “Good-looking women had roles in life and they were supposed to stick to them,” Jackie writes. “They could be movie stars, models, housewives, hookers, but God forbid they should skirt onto what was strictly regarded as Big Boys’ Territory. Writing a major movie for a major director was Big Boys’ Territory. And in their own petty little way everyone wanted to let her know that.” When Neil pushes to cast the craggy movie star George Lancaster in a leading role in the film, Montana acquiesces reluctantly—he can’t act—only for George to assume that she’s a secretary the very first time they meet. “I’m Montana Gray,” she tells him. “I wrote Street People.” “You did?” he replies. “My, my, things sure are changing around here. I could still use that coffee, little lady.” You might wonder whether things are actually different for women in Hollywood today. The statistics are still dismal: 8.1 percent of the 100 highest-grossing movies in 2025 were directed by women, a seven-year low, and the percentage of movies with any major female characters at all fell from 39 percent the previous year to 36 percent. But TV is faring better: More than a third of all streaming shows now have at least one female creator. One of those women is Lisa Joy, formerly a writer and co-creator of HBO’s adaptation of Westworld, a futuristic sci-fi show with a notably feminist question at its core: What might men do to women if those women didn’t seem fully human and the men thought they could get away with it? In 2019, Joy and her husband, the screenwriter and producer Jonathan Nolan, signed a reported $150 million deal to create shows for Amazon, including the acclaimed video-game adaptation Fallout and an upcoming TV version of Rebecca Yarros’s blockbuster romantasy series, Fourth Wing. Jackie would have liked the all-caps plaque on Joy’s desk—I’M CEO, BITCH—that greeted me in the Hollywood offices of her production company, a glass-walled room facing the Capitol Records building. Like Montana, Joy is impossibly glamorous and all too familiar with having people presume that her accomplishments are secretly a man’s. So much of Westworld, she told me, came from her experiences being the lone woman in what felt like male playgrounds. But, like Jackie, she’s convinced that writing a different world gets us one step closer to manifesting it. Post-MeToo, Joy said, she’s noticed some really positive changes, such as a junior writer she knows speaking out against a serial harasser whom no one had ever dared confront before, and who was subsequently fired. But reminders abound of all the ways in which things have stayed the same. “There are so many yacht trips I’ve heard about for male directors and writers,” she said. “I don’t know why they all get yachts, but there doesn’t ever seem to be a woman invited. But fuck the yachts, you know? They make you seasick. They’re no fun. Let’s make the reality we want.” I left Joy’s office feeling energized by her work, by her enthusiasm, by her determination. And then, as I was crossing Hollywood Boulevard, the universe gave me a palpable reality check: a 50-foot billboard advertising Louis C.K.’s upcoming comedy special for Netflix. On my last morning in L.A., I went downstairs to get breakfast in my hotel, and ended up sitting near two men who were discussing potential merchandising deals for one of their clients. “Makeup is really splintered; it’s a challenging category for sure,” one said. “But fragrance is booming. What about hair?” The client has a personal connection to hair, the other said excitedly. (Who among us? I thought.) The first man proceeded to expound on how hair brands are working quickly to address the issues—thinning, shedding—that can come from GLP‑1 use. The other circled back to his client, whom he talked about with such reverence that I leaned closer, convinced she was a huge household name. “Her pull on her community is so strong,” he said. “What feeds her passion is serving her community and improving people’s lives. She’s built for this.” Only by the end of the conversation, and after much furtive Googling on my part, did it become clear whom they were talking about: a Goth-makeup influencer on TikTok who has more than 6 million followers. You could argue that the content-creator model has democratized entertainment and broken down conventional gatekeeping, enabling anyone with a ring light, a front-facing camera, and a compelling personality to find their following. I don’t disagree. But it’s also restored youth and overconfidence as the preeminent characteristics for women on camera. Whereas what I’ve always loved about Jackie’s books is how they enshrine success and power as being hard-won and worth the hustle—and the wait. Midlife is when things get interesting for her characters, because they know the stakes they’re playing for, and they’ve accrued enough wisdom and battle scars to rival and even occasionally best the Big Boys. But the waning public interest in traditional storytelling (because you’re reading this, I’ll diplomatically ignore the even gloomier fate of the written word) has other consequences too. If you’re someone who doesn’t just love movies and TV shows but has felt genuinely changed by some of them, it’s hard not to mourn what we lose when our mass culture has all the bells and whistles of entertainment—hair, makeup, stars, product placement—but none of the curiosity about human predicaments underpinning it. Jackie’s work was berated during her lifetime by critics (“It starts off as nothing and ends as nothing” — Clive James) and high-school English teachers like mine (“trash”), but it graphically exposed a Hollywood full of sexual harassment and gender inequity. Not least, it imagined alternative paths for women to pursue. Now her books are mostly out of fashion; when I stopped by the Barnes & Noble inside the Grove, where Jackie liked to shop before going to the movies, the store had not a single copy of any of her novels. I was feeling despondent when I went for a last outing, to Mariposa: one of Jackie’s favorite restaurants, where she met for lunch each week with her closest friends, an elegant dining room tucked into the basement of the Neiman Marcus Beverly Hills. I had learned in emailing beforehand that Jackie was still a much-loved figure there. I was met by the store’s manager of brand experience, Mary Kate Steinmiller, who looks exactly like the actress Malin Akerman and used to be a fashion editor at Teen Vogue. She took me downstairs to Jackie’s favorite blue booth. For days, I’d been traipsing through Beverly Hills trying to find a little spiritual proximity to Jackie, and here it was, on the lower floor, by the beauty counters. Groups of women wearing Christian Dior tennis whites and Pucci wrap dresses chatted ebulliently over cocktails (it was 12:15 p.m.). Every table featured floral arrangements nicer than the ones at my wedding. Over in a corner, an older couple wearing acid-green designer outfits sat absorbed in each other. A table of women told me that Mariposa was their country club. They had known and liked Jackie, but quickly pushed back against the lingering stereotype of the Hollywood Wife. One was a high-powered divorce attorney. Another was a sports photographer. The restaurant was gorgeous (and don’t sleep on the lobster salad), but it was also clear what it meant to these women: community, affirmation, a clubby dining room of their own. Watching them, I felt as though what Jackie’s novels had promised might actually be true: If you can muster enough ambition and persistence to keep pushing through, the payoff could well be worth the wait. Ma Maison, immortalized in Hollywood Wives, is long gone, but in 1982 its star chef, a telegenic Austrian named Wolfgang Puck, left to open Spago on the Sunset Strip, redefining L.A. dining in the process. The original location closed decades ago, but Spago Beverly Hills lives on, with its twinkly lit courtyard, casual-luxe menu (Jackie loved the smoked-salmon pizza and wrote it into multiple books), and luxe-luxe prices. If you never get to go to the Academy Awards’ Governors Ball, which Puck has catered for more than three decades, this is at least a chance to eat stardust by association. 176 N. Canon Drive, Beverly Hills CA, 90210 Doughnuts and Hollywood physiques go together like deep-dish and Ozempic. Don’t let that keep you away from Bob’s, a 56-year-old stand inside the historic Los Angeles Farmers Market, where writers have come to eat and procrastinate since the 1930s. According to legend, Robert Altman used to have breakfast here, scribbling away under the slatted awnings. And the doughnuts really are great—pillowy and sugar-sprinkled. Fun fact: A branded Farmers Market tote bag costs $2, compared with $50 for one at the nearby Erewhon. 6333 W. 3rd St., Stall #450, Los Angeles CA, 90036 Is this tiny spot on Melrose a bookstore in the conventional sense? Not really, at least not if you’re looking for a popular novel or Belle Burden’s monster-hit memoir, Strangers. At Hollywood Books, the shelves are stacked with secondhand art volumes and reissued classics. Despite—because of?—its unfussed approach to business, the store has become extremely chic among the literary girlies of Los Angeles; at one point it hosted an event for the supermodel Kaia Gerber’s book club, Library Science. I left with a copy of Picture, Lillian Ross’s 1952 analysis of the clash between art and corporate interests in moviemaking, just to remember that things were ever thus. I can’t endorse the wraps or salads here, because I had only a sparkling water. But for eavesdropping like Jackie, you can’t beat the location—across the street from William Morris Endeavor’s boxy Beverly Hills headquarters, and next door to Cipriani. Sit, snoop, rubberneck. (Yes, wraps are passé, but power agents sometimes need sad-desk lunches that you can eat with one hand while scrolling through social media, just like the rest of us.) 356 N. Camden Dr., Beverly Hills CA, 90210 Funke regrettably did not provide any celebrity sightings, but it did furnish a gorgeous twist on a margarita, made with Sicilian-blood-orange bitters and prickly pear. The rooftop, after you enter through a jasmine-strewn trellis, looks out over the palms and offices of Beverly Hills, but from my spot at the bar, all I wanted to stare at were the pink quartzite counters and Chef Evan Funke’s elaborate collection of Amaro aperitifs. Jackie’s literary alter ego, the glamorous and hot-blooded Italian businesswoman Lucky Santangelo, would have appreciated both. 9388 S. Santa Monica Blvd., Beverly Hills CA 90210 Here’s to the ladies who lunch, and to playing one in this delightful department-store-basement restaurant, with its velvety blue booths and popovers accompanied by strawberry butter. The waitstaff are lovely, the diners’ outfits are extraordinary, and the food—lobster salad, crab cakes, tuna tartare—is opulent and calorie-conscious, and so good you’ll be wondering why the guests aren’t eating more of it. If I ever die and get to (at least) purgatory, I hope it’s a little like Mariposa. 9700 Wilshire Blvd., Beverly Hills CA, 90210 This article appears in the October 2026 print edition with the headline “Jackie Oh!” When you buy a book using a link on this page, we receive a commission.
