Trump Is Preparing for a Long War
2026-09-10

Trump Is Preparing for a Long War

The Atlantic | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
To hear the Trump administration tell it, the war with Iran is all but over. U.S. military posture tells a different story: Naval forces are being positioned for a sustained presence at least through the end of the year, keeping open the option of escalation. The Navy is moving aircraft carriers, its signature asset, around the globe so that two can be deployed in and around the Persian Gulf in the final months of this year, giving the United States the ability to hit both Iran and Iran-backed Houthi rebels in Yemen, who have recently fired barrages of missiles at Saudi Arabia. Meanwhile, the Army has extended the deployment of elements of the 82nd Airborne Division, which are already in the region, because of “operational requirements.” The conflict, now in its seventh month, has already lasted far longer than the president’s initial predictions of victory within a few weeks. And senior officials have recently downplayed the scale of the conflict in the apparent hope that it will recede from the headlines. Vice President Vance claimed to reporters last week that the U.S. is not at war at all. Soon after, President Trump referred to the conflict as “small potatoes,” though he has since termed it a war. After Iran targeted a U.S. warship, the Navy sank five Iranian crude-oil tankers on Tuesday, according to U.S. Central Command. With no talks under way, and no end to the hostilities in sight, the Navy is staying on a war footing indefinitely, adding to the severe burden already placed on the Navy and on U.S. munitions stockpiles. The unusually long deployment of the carrier USS Abraham Lincoln, whose crew spent 264 consecutive days at sea and dropped more than 1.5 million pounds of ordnance, was plagued with food and supply shortages, plumbing problems, and declining morale. The Lincoln eventually departed for Thailand, relieved by USS George Washington. The Washington joined about 20 other ships in the Middle East, including USS George H. W. Bush, another carrier that has been there since April. Now the USS Theodore Roosevelt carrier strike group is preparing to leave its San Diego base to relieve the Washington, which is expected to finish its deployment by the end of the year. In short, the Navy is planning to maintain two aircraft carrier strike groups in the Middle East near Iran for at least the rest of 2026, U.S. officials told us. The Navy has seldom mustered such a formidable presence there since the heights of the Afghan and Iraq Wars, and the move reflects the fact that the administration is considering a ramp-up of its military-strike campaign after November, the officials said. Talking to reporters late last month, Admiral Daryl Caudle, the chief of naval operations, prepared the Roosevelt’s crew for tough times ahead and a lengthy deployment at sea, especially now that Iran’s attacks on U.S. bases in the Gulf have made getting sailors to shore much more challenging. Caudle acknowledged that he couldn’t say how long the Roosevelt’s deployment would last and that it could keep being extended. “If we know we’re going into harm’s way, we should be able to project with a little bit more fidelity how long the carrier and the strike group is going to stay on station,” Caudle said, according to USNI News. “We do these extensions in little chunks, and the families don’t see any endpoint so they can plan their own lives—that creates a lot of stress for sailors.” He added: “I typically am not a big fan of extensions, and I push back hard on that. But the enemy gets a vote.” An extended conflict is not at all what the White House wants. Trump has been trying to extricate himself from the war for months, frequently frustrated that he has not been able to bludgeon Iran into surrendering—and angry that Tehran has continued to defy his bombastic threats. In recent weeks, the administration has settled on a campaign of economic pressure to try to force Tehran to negotiate more seriously. Officials told us that there are signs that the approach is working and that Iran’s economy, already battered, is further teetering. But economic pressure can take months or years to have its full effect. And the hard-liners in Iran have long shown a willingness to ignore the suffering of their people if it means tightening their grip on power. Trump hinted at Iran’s perseverance yesterday, telling reporters before boarding Air Force One that he now believes Tehran will try to hold out until after the American elections. Some aides think it could be even longer. U.S. Central Command told us it does not talk about future troop movements. Read: The real reason Trump wants economic sanctions on Iran A sustained economic chokehold has potential political benefits for the administration, keeping the unpopular conflict quiet while Republicans run for election this fall. But the inherent complication with that plan is that the status quo would likely keep gas prices high, which is why the administration has kept such a focus on the Strait of Hormuz, a vital waterway for global energy supplies. Iran seized it, and effectively shut it, in the early days of the war. Trump has privately told advisers that he is eager to claim victory and move on, but acknowledges that he cannot credibly do so while the strait remains largely closed or menaced by Iran, a senior White House aide and an outside adviser told us on the condition of anonymity to discuss internal deliberations. The president has signed off on military action around the strait to punish Iran’s efforts to thwart the U.S. blockade. The administration has also begun preparing for the possibility that the economic-pressure campaign may push Iran into ramping up its attacks, creating the need for a greater U.S. military presence, according to the senior aide. And though Trump would prefer to be done with the war as soon as possible, he has signaled a willingness to consider renewing a more sustained military campaign—but likely only after the midterms and once the Pentagon has replaced some of its depleted weapons supply. Right now, Tehran appears to favor escalation. In recent days, Iranian forces attacked commercial shipping, threatened to create a maritime exclusion zone in the Gulf (a nonstarter for the U.S. and other Gulf countries), aimed ballistic missiles at nearby U.S. naval vessels, and vowed to “take action against any threat, even before it is carried out.” Collectively, these moves by Tehran were sending a message: We are willing to go big to get a deal. The regime has also been preparing its own citizens for extended hardship, pushing national unity amid rampant inflation and shortages brought on by U.S. sanctions. “Their message is, The U.S. is using economic pressure to create another uprising in Iran, the prelude to the next big war,” Vali Nasr, a professor of international affairs and Middle East studies at the Johns Hopkins School of Advanced International Studies, told us. “They are trying to prepare the population for no short-term resolution.” Carriers are not the only ships capable of supporting the U.S. blockade of the Strait of Hormuz. But they give the United States something the other ships cannot: a mobile airfield capable of quickly launching sustained combat operations without relying on bases in countries that could come under Iranian attack. More broadly, the carriers are considered a form of deterrence, a projection of American power and intent. Yet they can also illustrate the limits of U.S. capabilities. As long as two of the 10 available U.S. carriers are near the Gulf, the Navy can’t sustain a carrier elsewhere in the world without putting a tremendous strain on the force. The other carriers are either in shipyards or resetting between deployments. Every time the U.S. extends a deployment, that jolts scheduling for shipyards by months. And it puts strain on Navy families and sailors. That also means no aircraft carrier in the Pacific to show U.S. determination to defend its allies and Taiwan against possible Chinese aggression. And no deployed aircraft carrier in the Western Hemisphere. “Once the administration decided on the blockade as its main line of effort, they also concluded that this would be a sustained campaign,” Bryan Clark, a defense expert at Hudson Institute, told us. “But keeping carriers in the Arabian Gulf for responsive strikes will prevent sustaining carrier presence elsewhere. We may see U.S. influence erode in places like the Asia Pacific, like Japan and Taiwan.” U.S. allies are already anticipating a less consistent U.S.-carrier presence in Asia, defense officials told us, with Japan and the Philippines discussing ways to bolster their naval forces in response to the threat from China. Meanwhile, the U.S. is relying on a different mix of ships for its operations in the Indo-Pacific, including littoral-combat ships that normally support the Middle East but arrived in Singapore for maintenance shortly after the Iran war began. The Navy has been trying to move some of those ships back toward the Gulf too. Sailors on the aircraft carriers in the Gulf will face a theater shaped by Iran’s persistence. Because of Tehran’s attacks over the past six months, the U.S. has lost the infrastructure to support multiple port visits for carriers in places such as Bahrain and the United Arab Emirates. For now, until the Navy finds alternative sites for liberty breaks, in Africa and potentially as far away as Singapore, carrier strike groups will likely get two stops during extended deployments, one en route and one when homeward bound. And with fewer safe nearby ports, resupply must be done from bases farther away, such as Diego Garcia, a British territory in the central Indian Ocean. “This will be the new normal,” one former defense official explained to us. Regular aircraft-carrier deployments last six to eight months. But the extensions in the Gulf are just the latest in a string of longer deployments over the past decade, as the U.S. has sought a robust naval presence in Europe, Asia, the Middle East, and now the Western Hemisphere. USS Gerald R. Ford anchored the flotilla that pressured Caracas in the run-up to the seizure of President Nicolás Maduro in January. Read: Venezuela seems to be going … well? Those longer deployments have ripple effects throughout the service, borrowing against future time that was slotted for maintenance and training. That has steadily eroded sailor retention and readiness. “When you overuse an asset, eventually you are going to pay a price,” John Pendleton, who directed the Government Accountability Office’s military-readiness work for more than a decade, told us. The Navy has seen the effects of such strains before. In 2017, after two fatal destroyer collisions in the Pacific just two months apart, Navy officials acknowledged that an intense operational tempo contributed: Ships were repeatedly tasked to meet demands despite shortfalls in training, maintenance, and manning, leaving crews overstretched and, in some cases, exhausted. Almost a decade on, a similar risk exists in the Persian Gulf.
The Atlantic
What Lonnie Bunch Told Me
2026-09-10

What Lonnie Bunch Told Me

The Atlantic | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Lonnie Bunch, the secretary of the Smithsonian Institution, was already slipping into the past tense when we walked together through the National Museum of American History in February. Occasionally, as we stood before an exhibit, he’d discuss his role leading the largest museum system in the world as though he had already stepped away. Even then, there wasn’t a question of if he would retire soon, but how soon. He spoke longingly of a future life in which he took fewer flights, slept in fewer hotels, spent more time with his grandchildren. He desperately wanted more time to write. He told me he still has more books in him. Now he will get the time. Bunch announced on Tuesday that he will retire at the end of the year. “I am extraordinarily proud of what we have accomplished together,” he wrote in a statement. “I leave with pride and sadness, gratitude and joy, and with enormous confidence in the people who will carry this remarkable institution forward.” Bunch said that pressure from the Trump administration played no role in his decision. That may well be true, but Donald Trump’s second term has played a disproportionate role in shaping Bunch’s final few years in charge. The 18 months that Bunch spent holding off the White House’s efforts to influence the Smithsonian reinvigorated his commitment to the institution. But it seemed to me that he was exhausted. He did not seem to relish writing memos assuring his staff and the broader public (while reminding the president) that the Smithsonian was nonpartisan and singularly guided by scholarship; he did not want to go to work under the constant threat of the federal government pulling funding; he did not want to have lunch with the president while waiting for “the sword of Damocles to come take my head off,” as he admitted during a talk at UCLA last year. He did so because that was what the moment required. He did so because he loves the Smithsonian, which has been part of his life since he first began a job at the National Air and Space Museum nearly half a century ago. But this, I thought, was not how the secretary of the Smithsonian should have to be spending his time. It all seemed like a massive distraction from the mission. I could not help but think of Toni Morrison’s observation during a 1975 keynote address at Portland State University about “the function, the very serious function of racism, which is distraction. It keeps you from doing your work. It keeps you explaining, over and over again, your reason for being.” Over and over again, Bunch was in a position of having to explain the Smithsonian’s reason for being: “At the Smithsonian, our work is driven by scholarship, accuracy and an uncompromising commitment to tell the fullness of America’s story,” he wrote to employees in response to this summer’s White House report, which accused the National Museum of American History of ideological bias. “As public servants and the keepers of this institution, we are charged with helping a nation find understanding, hope and clarity and as part of that duty, we are dedicated to excellence, reflection and growth.” Perhaps Bunch, ever the diplomat, would disagree that Trump’s social-media posts lamenting that the Smithsonian spends too much time talking about slavery distracted anyone from the mission. (Ditto Trump’s plan to place signs on the National Mall accusing a museum of being anti-American.) And although I know from speaking with multiple staffers that Trump’s attacks on the institution bolstered their commitment, time spent defending one’s work is time away from actually doing that work. Last year, when the administration first put out an executive order targeting the Smithsonian, people flocked to the Smithsonian museums, particularly the National Museum of African American History and Culture, of which Bunch was the founding director. Many people had watched what was being done to the Kennedy Center, and it seemed like the Smithsonian might be the next Washington institution to experience a Trump-world takeover. This summer has been a reminder for me of the singular role the Smithsonian plays in American life. I live in suburban Maryland, right outside of Washington, D.C., and this summer, our guest room has been occupied by a merry-go-round of guests visiting the District. Almost all of them have made sure that they got to the National Mall to explore the museums. It could be my little cousins marveling at the planes hanging overhead at the National Air and Space Museum; my friend from graduate school spending the entire day at the National Museum of African American History and Culture and feeling like there was still so much to see; my own children going to the National Zoo with their grandparents to see the giant pandas. The Smithsonian is a sprawling organization that represents the expansiveness (as well as the complexity and contradictions) of the world and the country we live in. Despite the diatribes from the administration, people yearn for that enormity and messiness. They don’t run from it. Months ago, when I spoke with the Yale historian David Blight, a longtime friend of Bunch, he told me that when Bunch left his post, he would have the opportunity to speak powerfully and personally about the moment we are in. “He could have a moment to really say something about the terrible consequences of these history wars,” he said. “And there’s really almost nobody that quite has his pulpit when he chooses to use it.” Bunch has already alluded to the fact that he won’t go quietly—that he might have more to say when he is no longer constrained by his responsibilities to the institution. Whoever succeeds him won’t have an easy task. Few historians are as respected—both within the field and by the general public—as Bunch. In the hours since he announced his retirement, I’ve received calls and messages mourning his departure, as if a beloved head of state had stepped down early. There is also a similar sense of concern. Will Bunch’s departure accelerate the White House’s attacks on the Smithsonian or quiet them? Does anyone else have the skill set to defend the museums in the way he has? Whatever is needed to lead an entity that’s caught the eye of a heavy-handed president, Bunch’s recent experience shows that the next Smithsonian secretary will need to make at least one significant sacrifice: their time.

A Playbook for Defeating MAGA
2026-09-10

A Playbook for Defeating MAGA

The Atlantic | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Subscribe here: Apple Podcasts | Spotify | YouTube | Overcast | Pocket Casts Donald Trump continues to insist that he is popular on the eve of an important election, even though everyone else knows he is not. “What I really want you to do is pretend, please, that I’m on the ballot. Just come and vote. It’s so important,” he said recently. In the meantime, the GOP’s first-ever midterm convention this week—headlined, of course, by Trump—has several Republican candidates sitting it out, probably because they pay attention to polls. The war in Iran, tariffs, and high gas prices have pushed his popularity rating down to about 35 percent, and even some of the MAGA faithful have been complaining. House seats that had been solidly red in Texas, Colorado, South Carolina, and a handful of other states are starting to look “soft,” as pollsters say. On paper, this presents an opportunity for Democrats to take back control of the House or Senate or both, and put forth some charismatic leaders of their own. But to do that, they would have to start behaving and talking very differently, Sarah Longwell, the publisher of The Bulwark and author of the new book How to Eat an Elephant argues. A longtime Republican, Longwell now says, “The Republican Party has to be defeated, and I think MAGA specifically, I want to defeat it and then salt the Earth with its ashes.” She’s spent the past few years conducting hundreds of focus groups with Trump supporters, and also swing voters, to try to understand better how Democrats can reach them. What she’s discovered is that many Democrats are mostly using the wrong approach, and they are missing a chance to speak to voters’ fears and deepest concerns. On this week’s Radio Atlantic, Longwell discusses how she thinks Democrats should talk about sensitive issues, such as immigration, and the words they should stop using. She outlines which Democratic leaders are doing a good job communicating and how to achieve what she calls “narrative dominance.” And she shares why—despite the contradictions, misinformation, and conspiracy theories she hears in her focus groups—she is hopeful about the American voter. The following is a transcript of the episode: Hanna Rosin: We’re less than 60 days away from the midterms, and in a rare moment of unity, the president and congressional Democrats have a shared goal: Make this election about Donald Trump. Donald Trump: What I really want you to do is pretend, please, that I’m on the ballot. Just come and vote. It’s so important. Rosin: Trump is so sure that he is the key to Republican victories that he has insisted on holding a Republican-convention-slash-Trump-rally in Dallas this week, where he will deliver a, quote, “presidential keynote,” presumably to help voters envision, when they’re in the booth, that he is on the ballot. But this whole scenario is true only in Trump’s imagination. As Democrats are well aware, and no doubt gloating over, Trump is not, in fact, very popular at the moment. His approval rating was at 33 percent at the end of August. And even MAGA Republicans are bristling at the war in Iran, tariffs, and high gas prices. Sarah Longwell:  Trump is as underwater as I’ve seen him in all of the years that I’ve been focused on politics in the Trump era, in large part because, much like George W. Bush toward the end of his time in office, Trump is fighting an unpopular war and he’s got a lousy economy. Rosin: I’m Hanna Rosin. This is Radio Atlantic. And that is Sarah Longwell. She’s the publisher of The Bulwark and author of a new book, How to Eat an Elephant: One Voter at a Time, which she describes as, quote, “the playbook for consigning MAGA-style politics to the ash heap of history (where it belongs).” Longwell is also a nerd for focus groups. She’s conducted hundreds since Trump first came into office. And she says voters’ attitudes toward the president have changed, even in just the past year. Longwell: In the beginning, when he first got into office, we would ask people how they were feeling about things, and they would say, Give him time. You know, Rome wasn’t built in a day. Joe Biden, XYZ—it’s his fault. Gotta give Trump some time. But now they’ve given him time, and they also identify very clearly that both the war in Iran and tariffs were both choices that Trump made himself. And so he is currently like an albatross around the necks of Republicans running in Senate races and in House races. I listen to these voters in focus groups. I was doing one a couple weeks ago; it was Florida Trump voters. We asked them to give him a letter grade, and C was the highest grade he got. It was just a bunch of C’s, D’s, and F’s. Rosin: Theoretically, this should be a great opportunity for Democrats. Longwell: I was looking at the Senate map 18 months ago, going, God, this map is terrible for Democrats. What are they gonna do? And now the Senate’s legitimately in play. Rosin: But—and this is a big but—just because Democrats have an opening here does not mean they are ready to jump into it. In fact, in her book, Longwell argues that many Democrats are not. From what she’s seen in focus groups, they don’t connect with voters in the right way. In fact, they alienate them a lot. We’ll talk about all that. But first, we need to know something about Longwell, who got her start in politics working for [Senator] Rick Santorum, who is a pretty conservative Republican. I wanna ask you: You’re a Republican. Why are you in the business of giving advice to Democrats? Why is that important to you right now? Longwell: I am not a Republican anymore. So as I lay out in the book, I grew up as a Republican and was sort of a John McCain Republican coming out of college. I was 18 when [President] Bill Clinton was impeached for having an affair with a woman, a girl, not much older than I was at the time. And so I got, in my very early formative years, slightly polarized against Democrats and then found [Senator] John McCain to be a really compelling figure as I sort of grew up into politics in my 20s. And I sort of hung in there with Republicans, although I was always, you know, I’m gay and I’m a moderate. I was always a moderate kind of Republican with some libertarian tendencies. But Trump was anathema to me, not just because he had a completely—I think he had a totally different set of policy beliefs from me. But much more importantly, as a matter of character, as a matter of liking America or, like—to have somebody running the country whose goal is to weaponize fear and anger to make us hate each other, I reject that. And I tried to hang in there for a couple of years and say, I’m gonna fight Trump from within the Republican Party. I tried to primary him going into the 2020 election, and tried to get Mark Cuban or [Maryland Governor] Larry Hogan or somebody to primary him. It’s actually why I started doing the focus groups in the first place, was I was trying to figure out how we could primary Trump. And then I continued by saying, Okay, well, nobody’s gonna seriously take this guy on. How do we get these disaffected Republicans to vote for a Democrat? And that was sort of my beginning of trying to figure out how to get people to vote for Democrats. As long as Donald Trump has the stranglehold on the Republican Party, there’s nothing in this party—in the Republican Party—for me. And more importantly, I believe it is an actively dangerous political party at the moment. Do I still believe in limited government, free markets, and American leadership in the world? Sure, I do, but I don’t see any reason why Democrats aren’t being more responsive on a number of those issues than Republicans are. I mean, the level of corruption they’re tolerating, it’s unfathomable. And so there’s nothing for me in the Republican Party, and I think the Republican Party has to be defeated. And I think MAGA specifically, I want to defeat it and then salt the Earth with its ashes. Longwell: (Laughs.) I know, I’m super intense about this. I think that Trump is really bad for America. I think he tries to weaponize our worst impulses. You know, he understands how to take people’s darkest impulses and amplify them, and he does that for his own power. I’ve never seen in my lifetime, and it’s hard to find characters like this in American history who, I think, ascended to the presidency while caring so little about America and her people. And I think he has made us all worse. Everything he has done has made America a less good country, and I’m desperate for change, and I’m desperate for Donald Trump to be a failure. My singular goal is that when we look back on the Trump years, everybody sort of agrees: That was a mistake. That was a failure. We never want to do that again. Rosin: So your driving belief is: There is a part of us that can remember that. I think there’s a lot of people sometimes who feel like voters are lost, they’re numb, they’ve forgotten how to be good. We’re hopelessly polarized. But that is not your view. This book is intended to find the place in the American voter where they can turn the country in a different direction. It’s essentially a hopeful book. Longwell: It is a hopeful book, and I wrote it because I’m confronted so often with the cynicism and desperation from people who think, Man, we’re done. America’s toast. It’s over. And our voters are fundamentally corrupt and bad. And that’s just not what I think. And what I think is that every—it’s kind of just part of the human condition, right? People have light and dark in them, and I tell this story at the end of the book, or I relay this parable. It’s, like, a Native American parable where a grandfather’s talking to his grandson, and he says, Look, inside every one of us, there’s two wolves. There’s a good wolf that represents truth and light and decency, and there’s a bad wolf that’s avarice and greed and hatred, and those two wolves are always in tension. And the grandson says, Well, which one wins? And he says, The one you feed. And Trump feeds the bad parts of America, and they’re there, those bad, dark parts. But the good parts are there too. And the question is: How do you amplify that? How do you get to a place where—both from a communication standpoint, a leadership standpoint, a political standpoint—that you are sort of feeding the good wolf? And not in a schlocky way. I’m not looking for a minister or somebody who’s perfect. We just need somebody who has the best interests of America at heart and is willing to fight for her and is willing to speak to people like they have the capacity for good again. And the other thing that I just I believe strongly is: If you think that America is finished, and you think there’s nothing left to fight for, well, then it is finished, right? If you don’t think we have agency to change things, then, yeah, it’s done. And so I really sort of reject and push back against this idea that it’s a fait accompli, and I kinda wanna shake everybody else out of that as well, because I don’t think it’s true. I think there is a lot to fight for. I think there’s a lot of good ways to do it. And frankly, I think, as we speak, there are leaders that are emerging that are coming closer to meeting the moment than we’ve seen in a while. Rosin: Okay, so let’s get into it, what the details are of how to do this. You found a lot of answers in focus groups. You’re, like, an evangelist for focus groups. Why? Like, what was the light bulb that went off for you in the early days that made you think, Oh, we can learn from these small groups of people who are sometimes frustrating and contradictory and all of those different things, but the answer is here? Longwell: Part of it was that Donald Trump was so jarring to me, such a surprise to me, that I realized that I was missing something important if America could elect somebody like Donald Trump. And so I went and started listening to voters, yes, in part because I wanted to primary him, but also I was trying to understand. And it was, like, one of those just scales-falling-from-your-eyes moments, as I listened to voters talk about why they liked Donald Trump. Longwell: Well, I mean, one of the craziest things that jumped out at me right from the beginning was how many people had watched The Apprentice. You forget about this, but Donald Trump had been living in people’s living rooms for years. And this is a really key part in why I’m optimistic. Donald Trump himself is somebody who has completely hollowed out the Republican Party and turned it into a cult of personality. So what happens when he’s gone? But he has a unique relationship with voters that other people can’t emulate, because they didn’t go on Howard Stern a thousand times, or go on Oprah all the time, or have their own talk show where they were in people’s living rooms as a carefully curated businessman that made voters believe, Oh, this guy is a good executive. He will know how to do these things. I looked at Trump and thought he was a buffoon. I looked at Trump and thought, I can’t imagine how somebody would take this person seriously. And I think it’s, like, an easy thing to forget about how Trump had fashioned himself as having a parasocial relationship with voters, even before that was kind of a thing. Rosin: And so what is the light bulb there? It’s like, okay, you learn that he’s fixed in their heads as a good businessman, despite the million facts that you could throw at them saying he tanked a casino; he tanked this or that business. It doesn’t matter. There’s no fact-check, right? Like, what are you learning? Longwell: So what I learned was that people were desperate for somebody who wasn’t a regular politician, who they saw as transcending politics. A lot of times, we try to put it in the frame of: How could these Republicans vote for this New York liberal? He gave Hillary Clinton money, and they were like, Yeah, no, that’s good, actually. He transcends politics. And also, one of the craziest things was, you know, you think about Trump as being sort of—people now say he’s far right or authoritarian. These voters saw him as a moderate because he wasn’t religious. He wasn’t like [Vice President] Mike Pence. He didn’t talk about abortion all the time, and in fact, voters would tell you over and over again, they’re like, Yeah, that guy’s not pro-life. You know, he’s probably paid for some abortions. And you just hear, for voters, that they thought of him as somebody who was more moderate, which is, I think, not how sort of the conventional framing was working. The other thing that was wild to me is: A lot of my friends or people that I knew in the media, they would say, Well, how do voters respond to this crazy thing that Trump said? And I got the opportunity in the focus groups to say—and I’ll give you an example; it’s one that I give in the book—is I was in Columbus, Ohio, the day after Trump had had the meeting in Helsinki with [Russian President] Vladimir Putin, where he sided against America’s intelligence community and with Putin, onstage, in Helsinki. Trump: They said they think it’s Russia. I have President Putin; he just said it’s not Russia. I’ll say this: I don’t see any reason why it would be. Longwell: Washington was beside itself. The whole city was, like, en fuego over 80 years of foreign policy being turned on its head. And I went into the group thinking, Okay, these are people who are gonna be Cold Warrior Reaganites. They are gonna be blown away. They’re gonna hate the fact that Donald Trump just sided against America and sided with Putin, and I was excited. And the moderator says, What did people think about what Trump said in Helsinki? And everybody just was like, What are you talking about? I have no idea what you’re talking about. And that, to me, the chasm between voters—they had no idea. Like, we had to explain it to them, and even then they’re like, I don’t know, I guess he has to meet with world leaders. The disconnect between what people out in the world cared about and what people in Washington cared about, that was a deep chasm. And it really was illuminating for me in thinking about: What are voters actually paying attention to? And the answer is: Things that affect them personally, is basically the easiest answer I can give you. Rosin: After the break: how the Democrats need to start talking differently, communicating with potential voters in a whole new way. Rosin: So let’s take an issue like immigration. That’s a complicated issue. I wrote down one of the quotes from your book. It’s from Matt from Indiana. Matt says, “The border issue brings drugs in, that’s a big issue, fentanyl. I mean, I don’t know how many times I’ve had to Narcan somebody because of fentanyl.” Can you parse that comment for me? And then, what is the advice that would come out of that comment? Because there’s a lot of confused information in that comment, and yet you would say that Matt takes it personally. Like, he connects whatever’s happening at the border with having had to Narcan people. So what do you do with something like that? Longwell: Well, in the group, I don’t try to push back on that, because I’m not trying to change Matt’s mind. But what I learned about immigration, and I’m gonna say a quote that you’ve heard a bunch of times from Donald Trump, but it gets at something very important. He says—when he was talking about immigration, he says, “They’re bringing drugs.” Trump: They’re bringing drugs. They’re bringing crime. They’re rapists. And some, I assume, are good people. Longwell: But what he is doing in that is: He is taking a bunch of different issues for people—people’s worries about drugs, people’s worries about their personal safety—and he’s saying, And immigrants are a threat to this. I call it “schema activation” in the book, but he’s basically making people afraid. He took immigration, he connected it to Your economic well-being is being hurt by immigrants. There is more crime because of immigrants. There’s more drugs because of immigrants. And so if you care about those things, as many voters do, Trump is able to tell you one complete story and put immigrants at the center of it. And I hear it back from the voters in comments like that. So what Matt is saying, it’s like, fentanyl isn’t coming over the border from Mexico. Rosin: My initial instinct is to fact-check Matt, is to just say, Matt, that’s incorrect. Fentanyl is not coming over the border. But I’m sure you would say that is a terrible way as a candidate to go about things. Why? Longwell: And that was the initial instinct of everybody, was, Well, we’re gonna fact-check all these things. Longwell: Everybody hired fact-checkers ’cause Trump was lying all the time. It’s like, Wow, we’re gonna fact-check. But voters were like, Well, who fact-checks the fact-checkers? Why should I believe the fact-checkers? And I think that the bigger thing to do is to recognize that Donald Trump saying he wanted to build a wall had nothing to do with a physical wall. It’s to understand that what Trump was doing was projecting that he cared about immigration, and he was gonna take it seriously. You don’t see any voters mad at him that the wall never got built, because the physical wall wasn’t the point. Interestingly, [Mayor Zohran] Mamdani, in New York, was talking about affordability, and he talked about his plan for grocery stores and transportation—he’s gonna make things free or low cost. I look at those policies and I think, Well, those policies are stupid, but focusing on affordability is very smart. And his ideas are like, Well, I’m gonna take affordability seriously. I’m gonna talk about the ways in which I’m gonna try to make things less expensive for you. That was the key. And so what politicians have to stop doing is fighting over the small stuff, and go right at the idea that voters care about immigration, and they think Republicans are gonna be better on it. And I think that they should, No. 1, you have to condemn Trump’s excesses on immigration, because he is committing many of them, and voters are frustrated with it, while also saying, simultaneously, as a contrast point, I will keep you safe. I care about your safety. I will make sure the border is secure. We will have law and order. We will have fairness. Because fairness is something that comes up in immigration all the time from voters, where they say, Look, I want immigrants to come into the country, but I want them to do—and this phrase, you hear it over and over and over again—I want them to do it the right way. And that is not an irrational thing to think. And so Democrats do have to find a way to sort of say, We care about law and order. We care about not having people come over the border, so we don’t know who’s in the country. That’s kind of table ante for this discussion. But also, We want immigrants to come here, because most Americans do. Most Americans just want them to come the right way. And look, there’s all kinds of racism and nativism and stuff that goes into these arguments, but as the broadest matter, and this is where I think sometimes Democrats really do—they’re like, Well, I’m gonna talk about the nativism. I wanna talk about the racism, the soft bigotry in here. And I say: Or don’t. Or just tell Americans, Hey, look, your safety really matters to me. We’re gonna keep you safe, and we’re gonna figure out how to attract the best and brightest to this country. We’re gonna figure out how to reform our immigration system so people can come here the right way. Like, that is how to talk about it. Rosin: One thing that stuck out to me in the book is when you said, If I could give Democrats one piece of advice, it is “stop talking about democracy.” ’Cause it’s true. Opponents of Trump do talk about democracy all the time, and it’s frustrating that that doesn’t matter. But it’s too abstract, or it just doesn’t break through. Longwell: Well, we talk about democracy because, like a lot of Democrats’ core constituents now, we’re all these college-educated voters who have taken a political-science class. And we understand that democracy is this term, that other countries have democracies too, and there are different democracies. Voters don’t experience democracy. They experience America. And the way that they experience democracy is—I can’t remember who I’m quoting here, but—it’s not by its roots but by its fruits, right? So when they experience democracy, democracy is voting. Democracy is freedom of speech. I don’t know; it’s just, like, the air we breathe. It’s the water that we swim in. That’s what it’s like for voters. And so when people say “democracy,” they’re like, “I don’t know what you’re—what is that?” Instead, you say, and I give this advice to politicians all the time, just stop saying “democracy,” and say “America.” And Republicans actually do this much better, right? Republicans make free speech the issue, not democracy. And that’s something that’s easier for voters to get their heads around. Rosin: Okay, let’s get practical. It’s before the midterms, then comes 2028. As you look around the landscape, are there Democrats that you see out there who are doing some of these things well—like, who are picking up this message, starting to use the right language in the way that you would want them to? Longwell: Yeah, there’s four people that I look at who I think are emerging as really strong communicators: [Senator] Jon Ossoff, [former Transportation Secretary] Pete Buttigieg, [Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez], and Mamdani. And there’s some other ones, but there’s a lot of Democrats that I look at that sort of come up from time to time where I’m like, Yeah, that person’s really good on paper. Like [Senator] Mark Kelly, good on paper. [Illinois Governor] J. B. Pritzker, good on paper. And I liked [Maryland Governor] Wes Moore. But there are people right now who are getting out there and trying different ways of communicating with voters. So Jon Ossoff, his background is as a documentary filmmaker, so he has people shooting him from underneath and from below, so he looks up there presidentially. He’s up there president-maxxing. He hit the gym, and he looks like a pre-brain-worm [Robert F.] Kennedy [Jr.], you know? He’s handsome, and he’s got a theory of communications. And then he posts the clips from his speeches. He does the speeches. Now he’s actively running, but he posts them on social media, and I’m like, Okay, that is a theory of the case about how to communicate and build relationships with voters. Pete Buttigieg is out there stumping for absolutely everybody. He stands in the middle of these town halls with a microphone. He lets people ask him any question that they want, and he engages with them sincerely and deeply and meaningfully. AOC understands social media. She understands how to be a real person. Anybody who gets her in front of a camera, she seems like an authentic, natural person. And even if you don’t like her policies—like, AOC and I probably don’t necessarily share a lot of the same policy beliefs, but I think that she is a good, kind person whose heart is in the right place about what she wants to do for America, and that counts for an awful lot with voters. And then Mamdani, like I said, I think he’s really showing the way on how you focus on affordability. Now, when you say 2028, obviously, all of those people are not gonna be presidential contenders. Mamdani isn’t, for sure. But I do think they are starting to formulate a theory of the case. And then I think there’s people who are doing a really poor job. So [California Governor] Gavin Newsom, I think, is doing a pretty lousy job, because he’s doing sort of a performance art against Trump, sort of mocking Trump, which I think gets you some attaboys on social media, but I don’t think is showing the American public a real way forward. Rosin: Okay, wait. Slow this down a little bit. I’m trying to understand what it is exactly that you’re reading, like, what your scale is. Because in the first set of people who you said were really good, like, why shouldn’t I have a tiny heartbreak that what we’re saying is: The main qualification for a good leader these days is someone who’s good at social media? Longwell: Oh, well, again, that’s what I’m saying. I’m saying that they’re good at communicating. And I think that for Democrats, they’ve spent 10 years with Republicans just out-communicating them at every phase. At the presidential phase, Trump practices narrative dominance. Republicans feeling shut out from mainstream media; they built their own media ecosystem that’s now dominant. Democrats don’t have something that corresponds to that. And so I’m not saying that they just have to be good at social media. I’m saying that Democrats have to go through and survey why it is that they are not connecting with the American public, why they have lost the ability to be a multiracial, working-class coalition, and the Republican Party is kinda eating their lunch on that front. What I’m asking for is for Democrats to figure out how to be a team together, repair their brand by having a set of policy ideas that they’re circling around, and to refocus on people’s material well-being, ’cause this is what voters want in the focus groups, right? When I go through the policy, we can fight about all the little stuff, but the main thing voters are saying is, Stop focusing so much on these divisive social issues, and focus more on improving my material well-being. And that means education. That means health care. That means my job. And I think Democrats can’t just say, Affordability, affordability, affordability. They actually have to put a plan forward that has a theory of the case of what it means to be a Democrat going into the 2030s. Rosin: Right. Okay, that all sounds amazing. Here’s what’s tricky about it. So if narrative dominance is the goal, what we’ve seen as the right and MAGA has built an alternative universe of narrative dominance is that it’s involved two things: lies and cruelty. And so I’m wondering, how you can create an alternate world of narrative dominance which is effective—because people might say they don’t want you to focus on cultural issues, but that certainly helped the right build narrative dominance. The famous “Kamala is for they/them. We’re for you.” Focusing on cultural issues really gets attention. So how do you avoid some of the pitfalls of building this alternate universe, and yet do it effectively? Like, maybe be aggressive but not be cruel. How do you walk that line, and have you ever focus-grouped that or seen it done really well? Longwell: Sometimes, people think that all Trump voters are alike, that for all of them, the cruelty is the point. And for some of them, the cruelty is the point, but not for everybody. And you say, like, If Trump built it on lies and cruelty, what do you do differently? And I’m like, Well, you could build it on the truth and decency. And honestly, that’s a little bit of a novel concept right now for voters. I think they’re hungry for it. I think they’ve been starved for it. However, it does have to come with offense. They do want a fighter, man. They want somebody, and not just somebody who’s gonna fight Trump. This is a little bit of a mistake. Yes, they are tired of losing to these bad Republicans, but voters tell me all the time, We don’t want to bring a pillow to a gunfight anymore, but also, we don’t want to be like them. And one of the big disconnects for voters is: Voters keep saying, Democrats tell us that the Republicans are an existential threat to democracy, but they don’t act like it. They don’t behave like it. And so I think that if Pete Buttigieg, and Jon Ossoff, and Mamdani, and AOC, and a whole bunch of these other people started doing things together, started pitching themselves as a team, started showing up together—just think about it for a second, how different it would be if Democrats said, We’re gonna show up for each other. It’s happening a little bit in the states right now as people repair the damaged relationships from these tough primaries on the Democratic side. But it’s not happening everywhere. [Representative] Jasmine Crockett’s not doing anything for [the U.S. Senate candidate] James Talarico. And that, to me, is a real mistake, because I think Democrats need to start showing up together as a community that draws people to them, instead of this crazy faction that is always at each other’s throats. Rosin: Okay, so that’s the future for the Democrats that you wish for. You say you wanna burn MAGA to the ground, also that MAGA’s dominant in the Republican Party, also that the Republicans are never going back to the way they were. So what is the future Republican Party after Trump is gone? Longwell: Well, this is why Democrats have such an opportunity, because not only do I not really know the answer to that question, neither do Republicans, right? They don’t stand for anything anymore. And when people are like, Well, who is gonna be the Republican nominee in 2028? You’re like, Well, I don’t know. Is Trump still alive? And how popular is he at the end of his term? Because the chances of Trump picking the next person or trying to pick the next person are very, very high. He wants to maintain his control of the Republican Party. It has always been more important to Donald Trump to maintain control of the Republican Party than to beat Democrats. And so I think that the Republicans don’t know what they’re gonna do about turnout without Donald Trump. I don’t think they know how to build that coalition without Donald Trump, and they’re fracturing between the “America First” wing—which is your Tucker Carlson, your [former Representative] Marjorie Taylor Greene—and your MAGA establishment, which are your [Secretary of State] Marco Rubios and [Secretary of Defense] Pete Hegseth and all of these types. And they’re all gonna try, and none of them are gonna be able to do what Donald Trump did, for the reasons that I stated earlier, which is that they do not have the same connection to voters that Donald Trump does. And so I think that Donald Trump brought so many new people into the system, and was so specific in the way he did it and the relationship that he had with voters, that the Republicans have no idea what they’re gonna do in a post-Trump world. And that’s why if Democrats can get there first with an affirmative vision, they have an opportunity to dominate in a way that puts MAGA in the ground for the foreseeable future. Rosin: Right. Okay. Well, Sarah, this has been great. I really appreciate it. I appreciate the book. Thank you so much for joining us today. Longwell: Oh, yeah. Thanks for having me. It was fun. Rosin: This episode of Radio Atlantic was produced by Rosie Hughes and Jacob Smollen. It was edited by Kevin Townsend. Rob Smierciak engineered and provided original music. We also had music from Breakmaster Cylinder. Fact-checking by Sam Fentress. Claudine Ebeid is the executive producer of Atlantic audio, and Andrea Valdez is our managing editor. Listeners, if you enjoy the show, you can support our work and the work of all Atlantic journalists when you subscribe to The Atlantic at TheAtlantic.com/Listener. I’m Hanna Rosin. Thank you for listening.
The Atlantic
The Election Deniers’ Long Game
2026-09-10

The Election Deniers’ Long Game

The Atlantic | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
In the anxious days following President Trump’s 2020 election loss—before his supporters stormed the Capitol, before his vengeful return to the White House, before the mass pardons for cop beaters—some Republican figures sought to cast his attempt to overturn the results as nothing more than a coping mechanism. Trump was merely blowing off steam, people close to the president told me and other reporters. “What is the downside for humoring him for this little bit of time?” one asked, suggesting that in the end, his antics would amount to no more than a footnote in the annals of presidential history. Check out more from this issue and find your next story to read. The January 6 insurrection a few weeks later would, of course, turn those predictions into punch lines. Nearly six years on, they look worse still: The election denialism that inspired Trump’s MAGA loyalists to attack the Capitol has metastasized into an animating feature of Republican Party politics. Trump himself remains at the forefront of this movement. Since returning to office, he has insisted that American elections are “rigged” against the GOP, fired Democratic election officials, threatened to “take over” voting across the country, and issued executive orders that would tightly restrict the vote. The president’s most far-reaching efforts have largely been blocked by the courts or Congress, at least so far. As in 2020, Trump in fact has little legal authority to interfere with elections, and this has given comfort to many Americans. But as November approaches, the most serious threats posed to free and fair elections no longer come from the White House itself. Instead, they are local, where much of the power over elections actually resides, and they stem from the continuing absorption of Trump’s Big Lie into our political culture. In statehouses and county offices, a cohort of election deniers has taken control of significant parts of the nation’s electoral infrastructure. Some of its members have replaced the officials and legislators who rejected Trump’s push to corrupt the 2020 race. A grassroots movement of Big Lie believers has meanwhile been organized and trained as poll workers or informal observers to sound alarms over local elections they deem suspect in any way. The November elections represent something new in American politics: For the first time, a coalition of partners at the local, state, and federal levels is ready to pull off the kind of election heist that eluded the president back in 2020. At the very least, the midterms could serve as a trial run for this coalition ahead of the 2028 presidential race, when much of Trump’s legacy will be at stake. Nowhere is the threat of election subversion more evident than in Georgia, the state Trump lost by the slimmest margin in 2020 and the site of his most brazen efforts to overturn the results. Trump’s hourlong phone call on January 2, 2021, urging Georgia Secretary of State Brad Raffensperger to “find 11,780 votes” to reverse his loss is the most famous example, but hardly the only one. By the time of that call, the president and his allies had spent weeks pressing local lawmakers and election officials to throw out votes, delay the certification of Joe Biden’s victory, and lay the groundwork for an administrative coup. On Thanksgiving Day, Trump had called Raffensperger an “enemy of the people” and falsely accused him of engaging in illegal election practices. The secretary of state’s security had to be beefed up—as did that of his colleague Gabriel Sterling, then–Lieutenant Governor Geoff Duncan, and others—following death threats. The Fulton County poll workers Ruby Freeman and Wandrea “Shaye” Moss were publicly accused by Trump-campaign allies of pulling fake ballots from suitcases to help Biden, a false allegation that elicited more death threats. In the end, the system warped but did not break under the weight of the president’s pressure. Georgia’s election officials did their job, affirmed the integrity of the vote, and followed the law. But the ranks of Republican officials in Georgia who resisted Trump’s election-fraud lies have thinned significantly since 2020. Sterling and Duncan are gone. Raffensperger is a lame duck whose power over the state’s electoral apparatus is fading. In 2024, he was ousted from the state election board he had once chaired. In May, he lost a long-shot bid for governor, garnering just 15 percent of the vote in the GOP primary. The state election board, which sets voting policy for all of Georgia, is now dominated by Trump acolytes. Salleigh Grubbs is one example. Prior to 2020, she had no role in government and had shown little interest in politics, but then, that year, she became animated by rumors that Democrats were plotting to steal the state from Trump. Upon hearing from a friend that a large truck from a paper-shredding company was parked outside a Cobb County events center a day after a statewide recount had occurred there, she sped over in her Toyota Venza. She took videos of the truck and pursued it when it left, later likening the action to a scene in Thelma and Louise. The truck had merely been hauling away empty ballot envelopes and other trash, but Grubbs remained convinced that election fraud had happened in broad daylight. She decided to get more involved, and won a campaign to become chair of the Republican Party in Cobb County in 2021, then worked her way up to an appointment to the board in January. Across the state, other Republicans with a new view of elections were making similar journeys. Ben Johnson, a tech entrepreneur who became the chair of Spalding County’s election board in 2021, has spread QAnon conspiracy theories, reportedly called Biden a pedophile, and asserted numerous times that fraud played a role in the 2020 election. Danny Bartlett, the chair of the Ware County election board since 2023, recently posted an image of Joseph Stalin on Substack, along with his apocryphal quote “It’s not who votes that counts, it’s who counts the votes.” On the five-person state election board, Grubbs joined Janice Johnston, a retired doctor who had become obsessed with election policy after the 2020 race, and Janelle King, a Republican media personality who had been appointed to the board in 2024. That year, King and Johnston had pushed for rule changes that would have given Trump more opportunities to challenge the results if he lost the state to Kamala Harris. During an August 2024 campaign rally in Atlanta, Trump singled out the right-wing members of the board, heralding them as “pitbulls fighting for honesty, transparency, and victory.” Johnston later resigned but was replaced by another MAGA Republican, ensuring that three of the five seats on the board would still be held by Trump allies. Since 2021, the board has been granted (or, in some cases, seized) broad powers over Georgia’s voting process, including the authority to assume control of elections in counties it deems to be administering the vote poorly. King has not ruled out the idea of the board taking over election administration in Fulton County, home to Atlanta and the epicenter of the state’s Democratic base. The question in Georgia today is less whether its current officials could resist Trump’s pressure again, and more what some of them might do on their own, without any presidential pressure at all. When the FBI raided a Fulton County election warehouse in January, seeking evidence of fraud in the 2020 vote, it cited the work of the state election board in its search-warrant affidavit. Johnston, King, and Grubbs filed an amicus brief in support of the operation. John Fervier, the board’s chair and a lifelong Republican, has sometimes found himself siding with the board’s lone Democrat to try to rein in the right-wing contingent, though many of his efforts have been futile. “Our board has at times gotten political when it shouldn’t be political,” he told me, carefully, in May. He said he was exasperated by what appeared to be an endless crusade by politicians to validate the president’s claims about his loss to Biden. “These people want to keep pointing back to 2020, and my thing is, to what end? ” We met inside the same Fulton County election warehouse that FBI agents had entered months earlier, wearing tactical vests and carting away more than 650 boxes of ballots and other materials. While they blocked off the entrance with crime-scene tape and trawled the cavernous 600,000-square-foot building for evidence, Grubbs was outside on a video call with Steve Bannon for an episode of his War Room podcast. She referred to the moment as “surreal” and “wonderful,” as election-denying activists posed for photos nearby. Indoors, Tulsi Gabbard, then the director of national intelligence, watched over the scene. Trump later spoke by phone with the agents who had conducted the raid, congratulating them on a job well done. The ultimate goal of the operation, and of all the theatricality surrounding it, seems fairly clear. The seizure itself likely caused more Americans to mistrust the 2020 result. It may have salved Trump’s ego. Any scrap of evidence that can be marshaled to suggest wrongdoing or fraud, however weak, will presumably be made public and spun. And that, in turn, may soften more Americans to the idea that election fraud is a real and widespread problem—ironically making the subversion of the vote easier to achieve. Even in Fulton County, election deniers have a significant ally. For the past two and a half years, Julie Adams has been a Republican appointee on the county’s election board. In the first months of her tenure, she declined to vote to certify any of the results of the state’s 2024 primary contests. There was no actual evidence of irregularities (and Trump had won the GOP presidential primary), but Adams wanted to further investigate. She was outvoted by the rest of the board, which by law has little discretion in validating the results. Still, Adams (who did not respond to my requests for an interview) has used her post as a public official to challenge the validity of Fulton’s voting process and has encouraged allies on other boards to do the same. Adams also serves as a regional coordinator for the Election Integrity Network, a nationwide organization of election-denying activists that appears ready to play a significant role in the midterms and the 2028 presidential election. In at least one private call with members of EIN, she has discussed changing state laws to give local officials more leeway to delay or reject certification of races. The certification of election results by local officials has traditionally happened without controversy, but Trump’s 2020 push to overturn his loss transformed this normally routine process into a new political battleground. Certification is a key means by which vote totals are finalized, and increasing the discretion of everyone involved would multiply the opportunities to raise fraud claims. The Election Integrity Network has been a major proponent of greater discretion—and in recent races, several like-minded election officials in various states have voted to withhold or delay the certification of results. Cleta Mitchell, a lawyer who aided Trump’s bid to challenge the 2020 results, founded EIN in 2021. It now has affiliates in at least 30 states, including every presidential battleground, and its volunteers are regularly in touch with Republican officials. The organization, which recently registered as a tax-exempt nonprofit, has raised funds while doing little to hide its political motivations. In an April 2022 interview with the conservative radio host John Fredericks, Mitchell (who did not respond to my own request for an interview) adopted Trump’s language as she lambasted Democrats: “The only way they win is to cheat.” EIN’s affiliates regularly attend state- and county-level election-board meetings, turning once-boring hearings about election administration—including issues such as ballot-drop-box locations and voter-roll maintenance—into contentious, standing-room-only affairs. EIN officials have also trained activists to serve as poll observers, encouraged volunteers to challenge voters’ eligibility, and advised members to lobby friendly politicians and go after lawmakers who oppose their efforts. This is a new phenomenon in the United States. In a system that relies on broad confidence in the sanctity of elections, the emergence of an organized infrastructure of activists focused on sowing doubt is creating fresh opportunities for malfeasance. Armchair sleuths and election enthusiasts have had dramatic success ushering conspiracy theories from the internet onto the agendas of county boards and state legislatures. In Michigan, Macomb County Clerk Anthony Forlini has pursued an audit of the 2020 election and suggested in January that a significant number of noncitizens might be on the state’s voter rolls, a charge amplified by an official at the Justice Department despite flimsy evidence. Forlini, who came under scrutiny for hiring a January 6 protester to train poll workers, is now the Republican candidate for secretary of state, a role that could prove pivotal in the 2028 presidential election. A 2024 investigation by the Center for Media and Democracy found more than 100 election deniers serving in election positions in eight swing states. There are likely more today. America’s highly decentralized elections have often been touted as a strength, making subversion by foreign adversaries or domestic cabals difficult. But many of the local-level positions that help administer our elections have low barriers to entry—the people who fill them are often appointed by local party bosses, county commissioners, or state election officials, with little public scrutiny. These roles are vulnerable to takeover by mass movements, especially those allied with bad actors or extreme partisans looking to upend the system from the inside. The hyperpartisan politics of the past several years have only increased this vulnerability. In December 2024, for instance, just weeks before the Democrat Josh Stein was set to be sworn in as governor of North Carolina, Republican legislators transferred the authority to appoint the chair of each of the 100 county election boards and fill a majority of the state election board’s seats from the governor to the incoming Republican state auditor. Every one of these boards is now dominated by Republicans, several election deniers among them, and the state has recently moved to oust nonpartisan election supervisors, challenge the eligibility of thousands of voters, and make it easier to throw out mail-in votes. These moves have come as Democrats vie to flip a Senate seat in what could be a close race. Many states, including Florida, Ohio, Kansas, and Utah, have passed laws that mirror those pushed by EIN—expanding voter-identification requirements, restricting mail-in voting, making it easier to remove people from voter rolls. Some of the provisions also resemble those in the federal SAVE America Act, championed by Trump, which if passed could disenfranchise, or at least confuse, millions of voters. The advocates who support these changes typically describe them as commonsense measures designed to enhance election security and root out fraud (even though evidence of actual voter fraud in modern U.S. elections is minuscule). Trump often dispenses with the pretense. Last year, he asserted that Republicans would “pick up 100 more seats” if his proposed bans on electronic voting machines and mail-in ballots were enacted. Even if he takes no additional action before November, the president has already done a lot to interfere with the upcoming election. He has punished officials who validated the accuracy of the 2020 election, encouraged criminal investigations into Democratic lawmakers who are up for reelection, and sought to steer taxpayer dollars to compensate January 6 rioters and other supporters convicted of crimes linked to his Big Lie—actions that all seem more likely to foster election tampering than deter it. The Justice Department began demanding in May 2025 that states hand over their voter rolls (many have refused), and the White House has neutered federal offices responsible for preventing actual voter fraud. And, of course, the president has used the bully pulpit like no other previous American leader to undermine confidence in the country’s elections. More than 100 times in the past year alone, Trump has claimed that the 2020 election was stolen, often likening American voting to that of a “Third World country.” (He does this apparently without considering what it implies about the integrity of his own two presidential victories.) His allies’ proposals to station immigration enforcers or military troops outside polling sites, which Trump has publicly declined to rule out, could have an impact even if the president does not make good on them. If voters fear chaos or intimidation, some will stay away from the polls. Abigail Jackson, a White House spokesperson, defended the president’s efforts. Trump, she told me in a statement, “is committed to ensuring that Americans have full confidence in the administration of elections, and that includes totally accurate and up-to-date voter rolls free of errors and unlawfully registered non-citizen voters.” She added that Trump was simply making good on a “campaign pledge” to millions of his supporters who agree with his views on elections. Trump’s efforts to rile his base will no doubt intensify between now and Election Day. A substantial majority of Republicans already believe that the 2020 election was stolen, that large numbers of illegitimate ballots are cast by noncitizens, and that mail-in ballots are rife with fraud. Networks of local activists will be on the lookout for any developments—rumors, photos, stray comments by election officials—that might seem to undermine the integrity of the vote. As Election Day unfolds, some MAGA electoral officials are likely to join in, significantly raising the temperature. It is possible that Trump might use the resulting clamor as a pretext to seize voting machines in key counties, breaking the chain of custody for lawful ballots and bringing the count—at least as a practical matter—under the control of his administration. In January, Trump told The New York Times that he regretted not doing so back in 2020. It is likewise possible that local or state officials could agree to “find” votes, or to discard them, though it would be difficult for any single official to change a ballot count without leaving an obvious trail. The system is too well built for that. But decisively changing the vote count would not be the only—or even the most likely—path to an illegitimate result for a key Senate seat or a handful of seats in the House. Instead, the president’s allies might hope to prevail by fomenting enough distrust in the results to change the terrain on which the election is decided. Well-connected activists are prepared this year to push for extending certification deadlines and ordering independent vote audits. Trump and his allies have openly entertained proposals to use security concerns as a pretext for nationalizing elections. Once vote tallies are rendered as tainted or unknowable, partisan actors in state capitols and Congress could play an outsize role in determining the result. How would all of this ultimately get resolved? The historians, election lawyers, and constitutional scholars I spoke with struggled to come up with straightforward predictions, in part because the country has never had such a confluence of election-denying officials in power at so many levels of government. Close or contested races for just a few House and Senate seats could leave control of Congress in doubt long after Election Day. Amid loud and continuing uncertainties, the Republican-led Congress could opt to exercise rarely used constitutional powers to override the voters’ will and choose the winners of contested races. The courts would likely have a say, but in an environment so thick with mistrust and confusion on fundamentally political matters, they might be more likely to defer to politicians. And the law is fairly clear that the House and Senate have the final authority in determining whom to seat in their chambers. If this playbook sounds familiar, it is because Trump and his allies pursued the same tactics in 2020, ultimately failing to persuade Congress and the courts to supplant the vote. Today, their chances of succeeding would be far higher. Many election officials hope for decisive results in November that leave little doubt about the will of the voters; landslides are hard to challenge, and even in swing states and districts, there may be a lot of them this year. Beyond that, key guardrails are still standing. Courts have largely frowned on Trump’s efforts to change voting rules. So far, Senate Republicans have opted against ripping up the filibuster to pass the SAVE America Act. In Georgia, Raffensperger will still be in office on Election Day. And across the country, elements of civil society are more prepared for disorder than they were six years ago. The American Civil Liberties Union, for example, has been holding regular training sessions so its members and volunteers can respond to attempts to tamper with the voting process, covering dozens of scenarios. David Becker, the executive director of the Center for Election Innovation & Research, told me that an army of lawyers is standing ready to help local election administrators beat back pressure campaigns. “We’ve recruited over 10,000 attorneys all over the country who are willing to serve pro bono,” he said. Still, it’s not hard to see which way the tide is flowing. “Under the best of situations and circumstances, preparing for an election is a very exacting process,” Mark Earley, the election supervisor in Leon County, Florida, told me, noting that administrative staff have to plan for weather disruptions, power outages, and any number of other possible events so that they can conduct a seamless and fully accountable process for voters. But now there’s more to prepare for: online mobs, and real ones, incited by rumors; potentially unreliable voices from the inside. There’s more pressure on election workers too. Recently, the Trump administration sent a letter to each state, threatening to prosecute election officials who fail to remove noncitizens from registration lists or allow them to cast ballots. And Trump himself signed an executive order in March calling on the attorney general to prosecute election workers and officials who give ballots to such voters. (The courts have since invalidated much of the order.) Some states and counties have gamed out how people would need to step up, on the spot, if various election officials were arrested while trying to prevent federal agents from seizing ballots or otherwise interfering with their operations. Many election workers have quit because the job is now so fraught with anxiety, Earley said. Several election officials told me they have exhausted the limits of their imagination in attempting to prepare for what may come. In Georgia, it was apparent even in May how activism on the right might lead to disorder in November. Conspiracy theories about voting machines were threatening to paralyze the state’s general elections. After years of activists spreading baseless claims that machines designed by Dominion Voting Systems were vulnerable to foreign manipulation, legislators had passed a law in 2024 requiring the state to overhaul its ballot-counting system. A leading proponent of the change was an eccentric 76-year-old man named Garland Favorito, who has spent decades challenging the integrity of the state’s electronic voting system, and who has collaborated with EIN. Favorito has also claimed that Israel may have been behind the September 11 terrorist attacks and that George H. W. Bush may have been involved in the assassination of John F. Kennedy. Favorito spent years pursuing a lawsuit, which was dismissed in February, to access ballots from the 2020 election in Georgia, arguing that he will be able to prove that the presidential result was rigged. He told me he fears that future elections are vulnerable to similar rigging and that we have a “national-security crisis” based on “forensics showing that our voting systems have been remotely accessed by foreign programmers and modified without detection.” Tax records show that his group, VoterGA, has received at least $385,000 from an entity called the America Project, which was co-founded by Patrick Byrne, the Overstock.com founder and a prominent election skeptic who urged Trump to seize voting machines after the 2020 race. At a gathering of election deniers in Washington, D.C., earlier this year, Byrne boasted of spending more than $100 million on election-related activities since 2020, much of it to support groups seeking to upend local election-administration policies. The problem in May was that the law banning Georgia’s current voting machines was set to take effect in just a few weeks, but amid continued controversies over paper ballots and other options, legislators had not yet established an alternative. Election workers were running a primary while also looking ahead to November with the possibility of a rapid and destabilizing changeover weighing on them. The deadline for a switch has now been postponed until after the midterms, but a new system still has not been finalized. To try to get some sense of what the elections in November and in 2028 might be like, I watched Georgia’s primaries, on May 19, from the Fulton County election hub. At the time, Favorito’s focus had shifted, at least temporarily, to a new potential source of fraud. Raffensperger, as secretary of state, had decided to deny election-board members access to the operations center where he would be receiving and reporting the voting results on Election Night. Because Raffensperger was also a candidate in the governor’s race, Favorito and other right-wing election skeptics began to publicly suggest that the secretary of state was attempting to count—and perhaps manipulate—the votes from the confines of a “secret bunker.” Grubbs, the state-election-board member who’d once chased a paper-shredding truck, joined Bannon’s War Room podcast on the day of the primary to complain about being excluded from said bunker. Speaking in front of a large Trump-Vance campaign sign, she agreed with Bannon when he likened the situation to a banana republic. Raffensperger rejected those allegations. On the morning of the primary, he told reporters that officials should stop making “wild claims and accusations” about the operations center. He suggested that those concerned about the vote-counting process should spend the evening at one of the county election centers where the ballot tabulation was in fact taking place. Jason Frazier, an election skeptic who has spent the past few years challenging the eligibility of thousands of Georgia voters, appeared to be following that advice when I ran into him at the Fulton County election hub on the night of the primary. He told me he had come to observe the tabulation process, but still felt that there was something nefarious about the operations center. “If the doors are closed and you’re trying to hide things, that creates a conspiracy, whether there’s something there or not,” he said. Just days earlier, Frazier had been hired as a part-time investigator by the state election board, the latest example of a self-made voting expert achieving official status within Georgia’s electoral apparatus. Fervier, the board’s chair, told me that Frazier’s hiring had happened without his knowledge. The MAGA-aligned majority on the board had gone around him to bring an ally into the system. In the end, little would come of the bunker conspiracy. Raffensperger’s office would accurately report the results of the primary race for governor, in which he was soundly defeated. But the night of the primary election was not without additional drama. When 911 reports of a gunman near an Atlanta-area school forced two precincts to close for about four hours that afternoon, a local judge ordered the polls to stay open late at those locations, giving voters until about 11 p.m. to cast their ballots. The delayed closing of those two precincts meant Fulton officials would have to wait an additional four hours to begin reporting results for the entire county.
