Inside the Fulton election center, I watched as members of the county election board anxiously flipped through thick books of voting statutes and called their attorneys to determine what the law allowed for. When I checked my social-media feed, I could see what was making them nervous. An online rumor mill had begun pushing the idea that Fulton County was intentionally holding back its results in order to sufficiently skew them once other counties reported. “Just one county in the core ATL metro hasn’t reported ANY votes…. Anyone want to take a guess which one?” Seth Keshel, a Trump ally and the author of The American War on Election Corruption, wrote on X at 8:57 p.m. “It’s always Fulton County,” Greg Dolezal, a Republican state senator and candidate for lieutenant governor, wrote at 9:49 p.m. “It’s time for Georgia to takeover the process. We will not have another 2020 this November!” He suggested that the state election board could temporarily appoint someone to seize control of voting there. The idea did not come out of the blue. Not only had Janelle King, the state-election-board member, previously floated it, but Republican Party officials had used the primary ballot to ask GOP voters in Fulton County whether the state board should “take over Fulton County elections to protect the integrity of our elections.” And although the results would have no binding legal effect, nearly three-quarters of GOP-primary voters responded “Yes.” Weeks after the primary, an audit would confirm the accuracy of the vote totals that were reported in Fulton County, and throughout Georgia, that night. By then, most of the skeptics had moved on. However the midterms turn out in November, they will likely tell us something about the integrity of the presidential election in 2028, and the elections beyond that one. Some of the president’s allies have suggested that despite his stepped-up attention to voting issues in recent months, he is much more focused on 2028. After all, the election of a Democratic successor could easily wipe away much of his legacy. It would also, presumably, end the government’s extraordinarily favorable treatment of his family’s businesses, through which the Trumps have enriched themselves by billions of dollars since his return to office. And it would likewise increase his legal risks. According to States United Action, a nonpartisan group that supports state and local election officials, dozens of election deniers will be on the ballots in November for positions including governor and secretary of state, roles that could prove pivotal in the certification of the 2028 contest. (In Georgia, the GOP candidate for governor is Rick Jackson, who has denied the validity of the 2020 election; in Arizona, the Republican nominee for that same position is Andy Biggs, who as a state representative voted against certifying Trump’s loss.) Many of these officials owe their ascendance to Trump. It would take only a small subset of them to disrupt the peaceful transfer of power. “If we have a corrupted election in America, I don’t know if we ever go back to normal,” Senator Mark Warner, a Democrat from Virginia and the vice chair of the Senate Intelligence Committee, told me. The country is built on the premise of free and fair elections, he said. “You unwind that, and you are in a very different America.” The truest danger this November may not be Trump attempting to cancel the vote or ordering troops to barnstorm polling places, but the smaller, quieter ways that democracy could continue eroding in towns, counties, and state capitals. As millions of Americans prepare to cast their ballots in the coming weeks and to settle in on Election Night to await the results, they should be ready to hold their local officials accountable for what happens next. Those officials, under the watchful eyes of the people who entrust them with power, must choose whether to use that power to reinforce the democratic system that has sustained America for 250 years or to bend it, little by little, further out of shape—until what remains is chaos. This article appears in the October 2026 print edition with the headline “The Election Deniers Are Now in Charge.”
The Atlantic
What the Aftermath of 9/11 Taught Me
2026-09-10

What the Aftermath of 9/11 Taught Me

The Atlantic | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
“But is it clean?” asked Elizabeth Berger, a 41-year-old mother of two. It was five months after the terrorist attacks on September 11, 2001, and Berger was testifying at a Senate field hearing on air quality in Lower Manhattan. Just blocks away, at Ground Zero, responders were still sifting through what they called “the pile”—eight stories of twisted steel and debris, spread over 16 smoldering acres—searching for bodies, bone fragments, even scraps of clothing. Outside, the air carried the acrid smell of burnt metal. Berger had lived 150 yards from the World Trade Center for nearly two decades. The complex was where her kids went roller skating and learned to ride their bikes. After the Twin Towers collapsed, increased levels of air pollution and particulates showed up across a wide area of New York and New Jersey. Lower Manhattan was covered in toxic dust. It had taken eight men in white suits and respirators five days to clean Berger’s apartment. She and her husband had thrown out trash bags full of toys and stuffed animals; handmade baby quilts; their clothes, sheets, and towels. They washed the walls. But every time the wind changed or she brushed dust from a shelf, Berger wondered what was still in the air, and whether it could make her children sick. “We don’t know what the right thing to do is,” she said. “Ours is a culture that’s based on authority, but there has been none. We would do whatever we needed to do if only we knew what that was.” That was exactly why I had called for the hearing. I had represented New York in the Senate for just eight months before 9/11. After the attacks, it was clear that the information about air quality coming from the Environmental Protection Agency did not square with what New Yorkers were experiencing. If the Bush administration wouldn’t level with people about what was really going on, then Congress needed to establish the facts and press for action and accountability. I served on a Senate subcommittee on clean air chaired by Joe Lieberman of Connecticut. I urged him to hold a field hearing in Lower Manhattan so that we could meet directly with experts, residents, and first responders on the ground. In the grand, century-old Custom House at the foot of Broadway, Kerry Kelly, the New York City Fire Department’s chief medical officer, told us about firefighters who had been exposed at Ground Zero and were now struggling with persistent coughs and shortness of breath. Schoolteachers described children suffering rashes, nosebleeds, and respiratory problems. Parents questioned whether sending their kids to schools was safe. Ellen S. Bakalian: The anniversary I wish I didn’t share with my husband Stephen Levin of Mount Sinai Hospital in Manhattan told us his team had examined more than 250 people who worked or volunteered at or near Ground Zero, and most had developed respiratory problems. He warned that without a comprehensive medical-surveillance program to track symptoms, we risked missing the people whose illnesses would persist or worsen over time. Again and again, New Yorkers told us they weren’t asking for certainty where none existed. They were asking for someone to take responsibility for finding the answers. Twenty-five years after 9/11, much has been written about that horrific day and its aftermath: the 2,753 lives lost when the buildings fell, the heroism of those who ran toward danger, and the extraordinary resilience of a city and the mistakes of a country under attack. I want to tell a different part of the story—one that’s still coming into focus even now, as New York City makes 170,000 pages of documents related to air quality in the days after the attacks available for the first time. It’s the story of the people who survived 9/11 and those who went to Ground Zero to help, many of whom would pay a terrible price in the years that followed. It’s also a story about what happens when government fails the people it is supposed to serve—and what it takes to make government work. I was in Washington on September 11, 2001, preparing for a hearing on early-childhood education featuring First Lady Laura Bush. I raced to the Capitol as soon as I heard about the attacks on the World Trade Center. My daughter, Chelsea, was staying with friends in New York, and I was desperately trying to track her down. Fortunately, she was safe. At the Capitol, an aide rushed to meet me and said that a plane had crashed into the Pentagon, only three miles away. The Capitol was being evacuated, she said. We had no idea what was happening or where the next blow might come. I made sure my staff got safely to a nearby townhouse, and I remember watching the disaster unfold on television. You could see the dust choking the streets and skies of Manhattan. I was already worried about the first responders working in that toxic cloud. The next morning, I flew to New York with my colleague Senator Chuck Schumer on a special FEMA flight and then boarded a helicopter that circled over Lower Manhattan. The television images I had seen the night before had not prepared me for the scale of the destruction. Smoke was still rising from the smoldering wound where the World Trade Center had stood. Fires burned deep beneath the twisted girders and shattered beams, and periodically flared. It would take three months before the last flames were extinguished. Firefighters, police officers, and construction workers searched desperately in the rubble for survivors. Our helicopter landed, and Schumer and I joined Governor George Pataki, Mayor Rudy Giuliani, and other officials walking toward the site. Occasionally, a firefighter would emerge from the dust and gloom, exhausted, dragging an axe, covered in soot. Some of them had been on duty nonstop since the planes hit the towers, and they had all lost friends and colleagues. I wanted to thank them and tell them everything was going to be all right. But I wasn’t sure yet that it was. The air was bitter, and the thick smoke made it hard to breathe or see. I was wearing a surgical mask, but the air burned my throat and lungs and made my eyes tear up. In the makeshift command center at the Police Academy on East 20th Street, we were briefed on the damage. Later, I saw a draft of the White House’s initial emergency national-security spending bill. To my horror, I realized there wasn’t one penny set aside for New York. It was an astonishing oversight. I caught the last train south before they closed Penn Station. There was work to do in D.C. New Yorkers were going to need a lot of help to recover, and it was now our job to make sure they got it. First thing the next morning, I went to see Senator Robert Byrd of West Virginia, chair of the Appropriations Committee. I told Byrd that we were going to fight to get emergency funding for New York added to the bill and asked if he’d have our back. “Consider me the third senator from New York,” he told me. Next, Schumer and I headed to the White House. We needed to see President Bush face-to-face and make our plea for New York. In the Oval Office, I told the president that New York needed $20 billion in federal aid—and needed it fast. He didn’t hesitate. “You’ve got it,” he said. In the hours and days that followed, some White House staff and Republicans in Congress tried to walk back this commitment. They wanted money for other states and priorities instead. But President Bush kept his word, and Congress approved the $20 billion for New York and the other states attacked that day. A week later, Schumer and I brought 38 senators to Ground Zero so that they could see the devastation for themselves. They met firefighters and rescue workers still combing through the wreckage. They talked with survivors and families. We wanted our colleagues to have this firsthand experience because we knew that New York was going to need their support for months, even years, to come. On September 18, EPA Administrator Christine Todd Whitman said she was “glad to reassure the people of New York and Washington, D.C., that their air is safe to breathe and their water is safe to drink.” Based on my firsthand experience, that was hard to believe. And rigorous indoor air testing had not yet been conducted. Sure enough, by early November, nearly 4,000 firefighters who had been working at Ground Zero were being treated for severe coughs. They started calling it the “World Trade Center cough.” I had seen this pattern before. As first lady, I heard from men and women who had served in the Persian Gulf during Operation Desert Storm in 1991 and later suffered from “Gulf War syndrome”—a cluster of agonizing illnesses linked to wartime exposure to toxins that the government had been slow to acknowledge. After hearing from veterans and their families, I urged my husband to establish a presidential advisory committee to investigate. He later signed legislation extending disability benefits to eligible Gulf War veterans with undiagnosed illnesses and improving how the government screened and monitored troops for similar risks. That experience taught me how slow the government could be to acknowledge long-term health problems, especially if they could cause political headaches. I didn’t want the same negligent approach to be applied in New York. On October 26, I read a report in the New York Daily News that heightened my concern. The paper reported that the EPA’s own testing showed elevated levels of benzene, lead, and sulfur dioxide. I wrote to Whitman demanding answers and a face-to-face meeting. I also started urging Senator Lieberman to hold that field hearing in Lower Manhattan. New Yorkers deserved the truth. And if it was as bad as it seemed, urgent action was going to be needed. I also began working with doctors at Mount Sinai Hospital, along with local labor leaders, to develop a monitoring and screening program for the thousands of workers and volunteers at or near Ground Zero. The doctors had seen firsthand how sick some of the first responders were becoming, and they knew that the full health consequences of environmental exposure might not be apparent for years. We couldn’t afford to wait for those illnesses to emerge before we started tracking them. By December, the New York delegation in Congress had secured $12 million in federal funding for the World Trade Center Worker and Volunteer Medical Screening Program at Mount Sinai—an initiative I had begun pushing for a month earlier, when I introduced legislation to establish a federal health-monitoring program for people exposed to hazardous substances in disasters. It was an important first step. But it was only a first step. The EPA continued to be unhelpful and at times untruthful. The full extent of the deception did not emerge until August 2003. An investigation by the EPA’s inspector general found that, in the aftermath of the attacks, White House officials instructed the agency to reassure New Yorkers that the air was safe to breathe, even though deadly contaminants such as asbestos and lead were present. The agency “did not have sufficient data and analyses” to back up its statements, the report said, noting that “competing considerations” such as reopening Wall Street played a role in their deception. I believe that President Bush himself genuinely wanted to do the right thing for New York. Three days after the attacks, he stood on a pile of rubble at Ground Zero, put his arm around a firefighter, and pledged his support. But members of his administration were eager to restore a sense of “open for business” normalcy. Alarming reports about toxic air near Wall Street would have made for inconvenient messaging. In my decades of public service, I have rarely been as angry as I was the day that the damning inspector general’s report was released. I thought of the firefighters working around the clock in poisonous dust and soot. I thought of Sister Cindy Mahoney, a nun who volunteered as an EMT at Ground Zero and prayed with weary workers—a few years later, she died of severe respiratory failure and asked for her body to be autopsied for evidence of the effects of exposure. I thought of every resident and responder I’d met with over the past two years, each with their own quiet stories of loss and heroism. The government had told them the air was safe to breathe. It wasn’t. And they knew that. Representative Jerry Nadler and I immediately organized a press conference on the steps of city hall, just blocks from Ground Zero, along with doctors and first responders who’d been lied to. I am usually someone who keeps her cool. This time, I lost my temper. “I know a little bit about how White Houses work,” I said, my fury rising. “I know somebody picked up a phone, somebody got on a computer, somebody sent an email, somebody called for a meeting, somebody in that White House, probably under instructions from somebody further up the chain, told the EPA: ‘Don’t tell the people of New York the truth.’ And I want to know who that is.” The Bush administration never really came clean about its lies about the dirty air. For a lot of people, it became just one more example of government corruption and incompetence. As time passed and more responders became sick, I pushed to expand medical monitoring and treatment. But media attention moved on, and the political will in Washington to act dissipated. In 2005, Congress tried to rescind $125 million that had already been appropriated for injured responders’ health monitoring. It took a lot of shaming and arm-twisting to get it restored. In 2006, Mount Sinai Hospital reported that nearly 70 percent of the roughly 9,500 responders it examined had developed a new or worsened respiratory symptom. That same year, James Zadroga, an NYPD detective who had spent more than 450 hours at Ground Zero, died after becoming gravely ill. His death made painfully clear what many responders and their families had been telling us for years: This wasn’t simply about monitoring people to see whether they might get sick. People were already sick, and they needed care. I introduced the 9/11 Heroes Health Improvement Act to provide long-term medical and mental-health monitoring and treatment. This was the part of governing that rarely makes for a compelling news story: the hearings and markups, the calls to colleagues, the negotiations over language and money, the votes that had to be counted and counted again. But every step mattered. We were trying to turn an emergency response into a lasting obligation of the federal government. It took years. The effort eventually became the James Zadroga 9/11 Health and Compensation Act, signed into law by President Obama in 2011, creating the World Trade Center Health Program and reopening the September 11th Victim Compensation Fund. The program exists today because leaders such as Chuck Schumer, Kirsten Gillibrand, Carolyn Maloney, Jerry Nadler, Peter King, and many others kept pushing after the rest of Washington moved on. That is the hard, unglamorous work of governing: staying in the fight after the cameras leave, listening to the people affected, lifting up their voices, and making the case for their needs again, and again, and again, until a promise becomes a program and the program gets results. There are many important lessons worth learning from 9/11. This is one of them: Government should be about producing results for people. It’s not enough to give an inspiring speech from atop a pile of debris (or, 25 years later, to rile people up on social media). Our leaders need to level with people, tell them the truth, and follow through with actual results that make a difference in their lives. Unfortunately, a quarter century after 9/11, this kind of patient, persistent approach to solving problems has fallen out of fashion in Washington. The Trump administration does not seem to have learned any lessons at all from 9/11. The president’s penchant for denying obvious truths is well known, whether it’s harmless facts such as the size of the crowd at his inauguration or deadly serious matters like the safety and efficacy of vaccines. But he fired or muzzled inspectors general across the government, so we’re unlikely to get a report calling out his lies the way the EPA IG did for the Bush administration on air quality in New York. The Trump administration barely pretends to care about whether government delivers results for Americans, other than tax cuts for the wealthy and sweetheart deals for friends of the president. DOGE took a chainsaw to programs that provide lifesaving assistance to people across the country and the world without a second thought. It was shocking, but not surprising, to see the administration gut the World Trade Center Health Program, even as the need has grown only more urgent. Nearly 29,000 civilian survivors have now been diagnosed with cancer linked to their exposure. Recent data from the WTCHP show that, for the first time, cancer cases among people who lived, worked, or went to school near Ground Zero have surpassed those among first responders who worked there. And the toll on first responders remains staggering: Just this month, the FDNY released a report finding that more than 12,000 firefighters have been certified with a 9/11-related condition, nearly a quarter of them have been diagnosed with cancer, and more than 600 have died from related illnesses. Every one of these survivors deserves the care our government promised. Instead, the Trump administration cut nearly 20 percent of the WTCHP’s staff and fired its longtime leader, John Howard. As a result, men and women who served their fellow New Yorkers in our darkest hour are now being abandoned in theirs. For the Trump administration, this is all business as usual. When measles began spreading in West Texas last year, the Trump administration cut staff at the CDC and sent mixed messages about the value of vaccination and treatment. Texas had more than 750 cases, more than 90 hospitalizations, and two deaths—the first U.S. measles deaths in a decade. We saw the same thing with lead poisoning. Last year, the CDC eliminated its Childhood Lead Poisoning Prevention branch, firing the entire 26-person team. The timing could hardly have been worse. Milwaukee was confronting widespread lead hazards in its public schools and had asked the CDC for help. City health officials had relied on the federal experts to help determine which children should be tested and how to respond to the exposure. After the firings, the CDC told Milwaukee that, because of the “complete loss” of its lead program, it could no longer provide that assistance. (The cuts were so egregious that the program was eventually reinstated.) Does anyone think the plight of those kids will keep President Trump up at night? Will the dinner crowd enjoying the rarefied air at Mar-a-Lago spare even a passing thought for the firefighters, police officers, and construction workers who got sick breathing poison air at Ground Zero? They might as well be invisible. When Trump is out of office—and there will come a time when he is out of office—Democrats and principled Republicans alike will have to rebuild the federal government and our democracy. We’ll have the chance to reconstruct entire agencies from the ground up. When that time comes, we should remember what the long tail of 9/11 has taught us: Policy must be about people. Americans who need help don’t care about ideology or who gets credit. They care whether government delivers results. So sweat the details, do the work that makes a difference in people’s lives, and stay with it until the job is done. Progress requires both urgency and perseverance. Sometimes you have to move fast. Sometimes you have to work patiently, step-by-step. You always have to put people first. From the September 2021 issue: What Bobby McIlvaine left behind For everything that went wrong in New York after 9/11, I’m proud of what went right. The roughly $20 billion in federal assistance for New York paid for the unglamorous work of recovery: debris removal, infrastructure, transportation, housing, and economic revitalization. Over time, it helped transform Lower Manhattan from a devastated financial district into a thriving neighborhood. In 2001, about 33,000 people lived downtown. Today, more than 70,000 people do. Elizabeth Berger, the mom of two who asked the Senate subcommittee if the air in her neighborhood was safe to breathe, was a driving force for that recovery. She spent the years after 9/11 fighting for a Lower Manhattan that was a “round-the-clock community” to raise a family and build a future. Sadly, she did not live to see what it is today. Berger died of pancreatic cancer in 2013, at 53. In the weeks and months after 9/11, the country united behind the words Never forget. Twenty-five years after that terrible day, we must make sure that Never forget remains not just a slogan but a solemn promise. That we remember not just the tragedy and the rebuilding, but the people whose lives were changed forever. I think of people like Debbie St. John, who was brought to the hospital as a Jane Doe after falling debris crushed her legs on 9/11. When I first met her, she told me she was determined to dance at her wedding, but doctors weren’t sure she would survive, much less walk again. I followed her recovery through the years, the setbacks and surgeries. I got to know her devoted fiancé, Gregory. And I was there, years later, at their wedding, and watched Debbie walk down the aisle and dance in Gregory’s arms. There wasn’t a dry eye in the house. There was hope and joy after suffering and loss, but it didn’t come from looking away from the hard things. It came from looking straight at the devastation and doing the long, hard work of healing. *Image Sources: Viorel Florescu / Newsday RM / Getty; Jim Watson / Getty; David Surowiecki / Getty; Spencer Platt / Getty; Charles Phelps Cushing / ClassicStock / Getty

Dark Energy Debunked? Cosmic Acceleration May Be an Illusion

The usual assumptiom is that there is no source of the big bang, everything banged. With enough mathematical wrangling, you could probably make current observations compatible with a centered big bang (far enough on the edge you might not be able to tell the difference). But it would be blasphemy to cosmologists to even try. "The usual assumptiom is that there is no source of the big bang, everything banged." That seems like it would require a lot of faith. They have enough cosmic coincidences to deal with, without a cosmology where our view of the universe is privileged in such a way it makes it look unprivileged. Why would science Satan do that? Nope. No matter where you look in the sky, on large enough scales it looks the same. There's no indication that things are significantly different in any particular direction. There can't be a 'source' of a Big Bang that looks like that. It has to happen everywhere or the sky would show signs that it was all accelerating away from a single point. What we can say is we don't really have much ability to figure out what happened early on. The conditions are too extreme and we don't have any way to model what might have been going on. That's not faith either, that's, "We don't know and might never be able to figure it out". This is the prevailing dogma, but has recently been challenged from more recent observations. (In defense of the dogma, we have very aggressively tested for preferred rotations with local experiments involving high energy particles. If there *is* a preferred rotation, its effect is vanishingly small, to the point of being functionally indestinguishable from flat spacetime at local scales, to an astonishing level of precision. This strongly suggests 'sampling error' for JWST data that follows) The observation is that on large scales, everything is moving away from everything else. No assumption needed. Although explaining the mechanism is hard, with the obvious one that the universe started off tiny and empty space filled in around it -- the big bang. The cosmic microwave background radiation confirms that the universe used to be much hotter, which fits if the universe was once denser (less space between things) with the obvious one that the universe started off tiny and empty space filled in around it -- the big bang. No, there was no "empty space" around it. The whole reason no faith was required to hypothesize the Big Bang, is that everything looks like that's what happened. Pick a direction (any direction) and the further-away stuff is moving away faster than the closer stuff. If you're looking for faith, it's here, but it's not that. The faith required by science is that our senses sense reality. The IRS Gave ICE Some Information It Really Shouldn’t Have. There Will Be Consequences for All of Us.

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
This is Executive Dysfunction, a newsletter that highlights one under-the-radar story about how Trump is changing the law—or how the law is pushing back—and keeps you posted on the latest from Slate’s Jurisprudence team. Click here to receive it in your inbox each week. The IRS arguably holds Americans’ most sensitive information, more so than any other federal agency, and for the better part of 2025, it quietly handed over that information, to the tune of about 47,000 records, to the Department of Homeland Security. This was done to aid in President Donald Trump’s deportation agenda, but that doesn’t mean it was legal: An appeals court this week found that the IRS violated federal law when it turned over the addresses of folks it deemed to lack permanent legal status. Now the agency and the employees who participated in the illicit information sharing could face civil and criminal consequences, including lawsuits from people who were deported after the IRS delivered their information to DHS. The IRS is just one agency that got roped into cooperating with the Trump administration amid its immigration crackdown last year. Under normal circumstances, the IRS is forbidden from disclosing Americans’ tax information to anyone, including to other federal agencies and even within the executive branch, something Congress codified into federal law in the aftermath of Watergate. However, there are a few narrow exceptions, including active non-tax-related criminal investigations. This is where Trump’s DHS began scheming, crafting a memorandum of understanding that took advantage of a loophole in a federal statute. The memorandum stated that the IRS could provide undocumented taxpayers’ addresses because a person is technically subject to criminal investigation if they remain in the U.S. beyond 90 days after receiving a final order of removal. The MOU was so controversial it drove former Acting IRS Commissioner Melanie Krause, whom Trump appointed in early 2025, to resign in protest last April, alongside other top IRS leadership. Nevertheless, (now) former Immigration and Customs Enforcement Director Todd Lyons wasted no time after the IRS deal was struck, requesting the last known addresses of 1.28 million undocumented individuals late that June. By July 1, the IRS was giving its employees the green light to process Lyons’ requests. However, according to the U.S. Court of Appeals for the District of Columbia Circuit, per federal law, that blanket request shouldn’t have been honored. Instead, ICE should have provided the IRS with specific information about its criminal investigations before the IRS shared any of its records, including the addresses DHS had on file for the specified individuals; however, that did not happen for more than 90 percent of DHS’s records requests. As the appeals court concluded, the IRS “never confirmed whether ICE request contained information plausibly reflecting ‘the name and address of the taxpayer.’ ” In fact, the judges suggested, ICE could have submitted requests with addresses like “ ‘Don’t Care 12345,’ or ‘00000,’ and still received a taxpayer’s address.” That’s not all. If it had followed the law, ICE would have identified a point of contact at the agency for each taxpayer’s criminal investigation. Instead, the court found, Lyons was listed for all 1.28 million records requests—which is “facially impossible,” the judges wrote. The agency also failed to provide a reason why a taxpayer’s address would be relevant to its investigation. The bottom line is that the IRS dramatically moved away from its long-standing policy of closely guarding taxpayers’ sensitive information and reviewing records requests on a case-by-case basis to “a mass, automated review of millions of records at the press of a button.” What happens now? For its actions, the government could “face steep civil and criminal consequences for willful disclosure of information,” in violation of federal tax law—but it’s largely up to Congress to decide if and how to exact those consequences. The House Ways and Means Committee or the Senate Finance Committee could hold oversight hearings to dig into what occurred here and chose to penalize the IRS by pulling its funding or imposing guardrails on its work. The appetite to do so likely depends largely on whether Democrats retake the House in the upcoming midterms. Even more significantly, everyone who was swept up in DHS’s probe of addresses and was deported or subjected to ICE because the IRS handed over their tax records now has standing to sue. It’s not clear how many individuals were deported directly from the two agencies’ MOU, but we do know that arrests and deportations across the country have skyrocketed. However, those who were directly affected and are no longer on U.S. soil likely face an uphill battle to pursue legal action against the Trump administration. There’s another way this could all come back to bite the IRS. The agency has for decades carefully built up trust within America’s immigrant population by pushing them to file their taxes, no matter their status, and promising that this will not trigger deportation. With a new tax season beginning on Jan. 1, “some people will be wary of filing their tax returns,” Martin Sheil, a former IRS investigator and supervisory special agent for the agency’s Criminal Investigation Division, told me. “From an IRS perspective, it is their mission to ensure and promote voluntary compliance with the Internal Revenue Code.” The whole situation underscores the IRS’s politically fraught mission, which for decades, Sheil said, heavily discouraged its employees from making any illegal disclosures—even inadvertent ones—because people need to feel confident that their tax records are being protected. “Then you get into a wrestling match between agencies and mission priorities,” Sheil said. “ICE feels like their No. 1 one priority, laid out by the president, is to scoop up illegal immigrants, and they want to use any means necessary to assist them in meeting their mission priority.” But all federal agencies are at risk if Americans suddenly stop complying with the IRS—a trend that should majorly worry Congress. Maintaining the confidence of taxpayers is integral to keeping the federal government’s lights on, particularly when you consider that undocumented workers pay an estimated $90 billion in federal, state, and local taxes, despite being ineligible for most social programs, including Social Security and Medicare. Even a 1-percentage-point loss among the overall U.S. population would slash federal revenue by $40 billion. Over the past year, everyone in the U.S., regardless of legal status, has personally witnessed or read about ICE’s heavy-handed tactics across the country and is now learning about the unlawful relationship the IRS struck up with DHS. That could cumulatively inhibit people from complying with tax law, and this ought to alarm Congress, the body that has not just the authority to step in and investigate how the IRS and DHS have been working together, but an obligation to do so. We hope you learned a thing or two from this edition of Executive Dysfunction. If you enjoyed reading it, please consider supporting our legal journalism by becoming a Slate Plus member! The episode also delves into how the court’s conservative majority uses doctrinal head-fakes to ensure that the president always wins in court. In the Amicus bonus episode, Madiba K. Dennie, of Balls and Strikes, joins Dahlia to unpack the justices’ attempts to prevent their papers from going public and analyzes the broader message that sends about SCOTUS. House GOP Leader Mike Johnson failed to pass an amendment that would cap the number of Supreme Court justices at nine. Friend of Slate Alexis Romero, an economic and environmental justice attorney, argues that this cynical move demonstrates that “Republicans are so scared of the electorate voting for judicial reform” that they are willing to try to sign away their own authority on the matter. Trump’s executive order commanding the U.S. Postal Service to suppress mail voting is not actually the first time the federal government has attempted to control what gets mailed to Americans. Friend of Slate Steven Lubet, the Williams Memorial Professor Emeritus at Northwestern University Pritzker School of Law, explains how USPS was similarly weaponized after a postmaster refused to mail pamphlets highlighting the human-rights abuses perpetuated by slavery. The trial of Lindsay Clancy, the high-profile murder case that consumed the nation for weeks, ended in a mistrial last Friday. The jury was held up by a single juror, who refused to join his 11 fellow jurors in finding Clancy not guilty. I spoke with a trial attorney to understand how common a hung jury really is, why the judge in this case decided not to throw out the one unruly juror, and what the prosecution should do next. Trump has continuously violated the rights and interests of individual states for his personal and partisan political interests, an impeachable offense, argues friend of Slate Frank Bowman, a law professor and a former federal and state prosecutor. The president has specifically gone after blue states, following an unprecedented pattern that amounts to “an abuse of presidential power sufficiently egregious and disruptive of constitutional order to merit impeachment,” Bowman writes. The Surprising Moment MAGA’s Hold on America Broke
2026-09-10

The Surprising Moment MAGA’s Hold on America Broke

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. Exactly one year ago, on a warm afternoon in north-central Utah, conservative activist Charlie Kirk was shot and killed, allegedly by a 22-year-old assassin who had set up a makeshift sniper’s nest on a nearby rooftop. Kirk was in the middle of one of the campus debate shows that had become his calling card during his decade-long career as a MAGA agitator, and as such, disturbing videos of the killing quickly spread across social media. If you’ve never seen the footage, I don’t recommend seeking it out. Kirk lolls his head back in a sickeningly unnatural motion; blood gushes from his neck; the crowd screams and scatters. It’s an astonishing act of violence, and Kirk was officially pronounced dead at the hospital two hours later. In the aftermath of the assassination, the MAGA commentariat instinctively sainted the newly martyred Kirk. There was good reason for this: Kirk was a victim of political violence, and victims of political violence are generally reified in the public record. At long last, Donald Trump had a bloody shirt to wave about. But when I survey the legacy of that fateful day in Utah from the vantage point of 2026, something else becomes clear. What conservatives thought they might harvest from the death of Kirk—a soft-power cultural mandate, broad bipartisan empathy, perhaps an assertion that it was liberals who were the real psychopaths in the room—never came to pass. Instead, ironically, I’ve concluded that Kirk’s killing represented the tipping point of Peak MAGAdom, a breaking of a certain fever. It had been less than a year since Trump had retaken the presidency—and unlike in 2017, in 2025 the masses were grappling with whether Trump’s movement was culturally ascendent. Kirk’s murder proved that it was not. How am I so sure about this? Well, for one thing, on the first anniversary of Charlie Kirk’s death, he has become, by far, the biggest meme on the internet. Where do we even start? How about the most offensive thing I’ve ever seen—we can work backward from there. Here is an A.I.-hewn video of Charlie Kirk at that pivotal Utah debate show. Portentous clouds gather overhead. The bullet is fired, and, miraculously, it is caught by someone inches away from its target. The camera pulls back to reveal who Kirk’s savior is. It’s Jeffrey Epstein. He and Kirk form a conga line in celebration. (This rendering was posted by X user @GlobalNewsMontr. As of this writing, it has been viewed more than 1.3 million times.) Zooming out further, here is a subreddit—called r/kirkified—that was created on Nov. 2, 2025, less than two months after the assassination. Inside, there are hundreds—thousands?—of images edited to paste Kirk’s distinctive gestalt into otherwise unrelated scenes. Here is Kirk as Napoleon Bonaparte. Here is Kirk as Gordon Freeman from the video game franchise Half-Life. Here is Kirk superimposed on the iconic McLovin ID from Superbad. “Kirkification”—and its syntactical variants—has gained real salience in the lexicon. You see it everywhere, with remarkable leaps in scale. For instance: Here is a Kirkified version of a scene from an Odyssey, where … well, where an unlucky soldier takes an arrow to the neck. (It’s sitting at 5.3 million views.) I could go on and on. Getting “Kirked” is now zoomer slang for getting killed. (It has also been deployed as a euphemism for oral sex. I don’t endorse any of this by the way—I am just reporting the facts.) Elsewhere, Kirk’s legacy has been desecrated to the point that it has shed even the thinnest connection to its original context. On a recent podcast clip, I heard the 22-year-old comedian and actress Ivy Wolk use the term “lowkirkuinely”—a portmanteau of “low-key” and “genuinely”—alloyed with the podcaster’s name. (“Just the name Charlie Kirk can send people of my generation into a fit of giggles,” said Wolk, explaining the humor.) You are free to find all of this to be pretty ghoulish. I can’t really blame you. Kirk left behind a widow and two young children. He was only 31 years old. The internet’s humor has a certain nihilism to it, and it’s fair for anyone to be turned off by that. (Remember, 9/11—which is also about to celebrate a big anniversary—is the subject of countless questionable memes as well.) But I do think something else is going on here, beyond typical online stuff. The derisiveness expressed toward Kirk—the permission so many people have given themselves to dance on his grave—feels, to me, in direct conversation with how Americans were instructed to process his untimely death, and that is one of the great unforced errors of the late and febrile Trump era. Seriously, remember what life felt like in the summer of 2025? It was the high-water mark of MAGAdom. Trump was back in office with his first and only popular vote victory under his belt. The tide of wokeness had been battled back into the abyss; companies and institutions cowered in front of this newly cemented moral majority. Liberals stared at themselves in the mirror, wondering how it all went so wrong, and some Democratic Party elites seemed eager to break right. (Here’s where I’ll remind you that shortly after the 2024 election, Gavin Newsom invited Kirk to his podcast studio.) So naturally, in the white heat of MAGA’s dominance, Kirk’s death resulted in him being feted like he was Nelson Mandela. Trump personally announced Kirk’s passing on his Twitter account and blessed him with the auspices of a state funeral. Republicans attempted to cobble together votes allowing the man to lie in state within the Capitol Rotunda; statues of Kirk were commissioned in MAGA strongholds; Vladimir Putin toasted his defense of “traditional values.” More importantly, the administration used Kirk to assert its political ambition, which we all thought was nearing its apogee. Stephen Miller announced a dubious crackdown on “far-left terror” networks, which felt like a naked extension of his paramilitary apparatus. And who could forget the whole Jimmy Kimmel debacle? The late-night host was yanked off TV for a spell, under direct pressure from the Federal Communications Commission—all because he had the temerity to make a mild joke at Kirk’s expense. It was those overreactions that, in retrospect, led to the latter-day blaspheming of Kirk—meted out in caustic memes, every day. Because no matter the horrific circumstances of his murder, Charlie Kirk is not Nelson Mandela. He went to his grave as the same man he always was: a bigoted podcaster who gleefully stoked hatred; someone who has antagonized innumerable minority groups, who helped bus rioters to the “Stop the Steal” rally, who, in fact, spent his last moments on Earth making a flagrantly racist comment about gang violence. None of that justified his fate—not even close—but it did make Kirk an easily recognizable archetype in the MAGA strata: an individual well-versed in the shabby right-wing grift, a gigantic microphone in front of his smirking face, permeating the airwaves with his poisonous worldview. The sacredness prescribed by the Trump administration—asking us to reserve the same solemnity for Kirk as we did for popes, firefighters, and veterans, which the president so rarely offers his own enemies—was a bridge too far. It turns out that millions of Americans refused to lie to themselves about who Kirk was. And as the MAGA machine attempted to inculcate respect for the departed podcaster through raw vehicles of coercion, the spirit of rebellion grew more potent. Ultimately, I think that is what a Kirkified meme attempts to express: the right to memorialize the man in any way we see fit. Two months after Kirk’s passing, a song titled “We Are Charlie Kirk” was uploaded by the YouTube channel “Authentic Music.” It was presented as a tribute to the late podcaster; the chorus speaks of “carrying the flame,” and “honoring his name.” It was also, clearly, the product of A.I., and like all A.I. music, the results were turgid, saccharine, and spiritually vacant—reminiscent of an Imagine Dragons B-side lost to time. Unsurprisingly, the song was quickly repurposed as a Kirk meme, and in that context, its lyrics have taken on a completely different meaning. The memers will ensure that the world never forgets who Charlie Kirk really was. They will shatter the MAGA propaganda machine. They will carry the flame. He Quit a Major Tech Company Because He Thinks It’s Risking Human Extinction. We Should at Least Hear Him Out, Right?
2026-09-09

He Quit a Major Tech Company Because He Thinks It’s Risking Human Extinction. We Should at Least Hear Him Out, Right?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-09

Visit Original Website
Sign up for the Slatest to get the most insightful analysis, criticism, and advice out there, delivered to your inbox daily. People have worried about artificial intelligence destroying humanity since A.I. existed only in science fiction. But the latest warning comes from someone worth listening to: A researcher at Anthropic, which makes the chatbot Claude, just quit out of fear that the industry is barreling toward creating A.I. systems that could bring about Armageddon. Until yesterday, Jacob Coxon trained Anthropic’s new A.I. models. He previously worked for OpenAI, a competitor behind ChatGPT, but left earlier this year because Anthropic has a reputation for caring more about A.I. safety. Yet the 27-year-old Coxon ended up disappointed. “Neither company is acting responsibly,” he wrote on X yesterday. “They are racing straight to self-improving superintelligence and gambling with our lives.” The fear is that A.I. models could get so sophisticated that they exceed human intelligence, a possibility known as “artificial superintelligence.” An A.I. that can refine its own code—what experts call recursive self-improvement—could also outsmart human efforts to control it. Some researchers worry that a sufficiently advanced A.I. could evade human oversight and cause catastrophic harm by, say, launching cyberattacks on critical infrastructure or designing chemical weapons. He’s part of a wave of warnings about unchecked A.I. systems, including from leading industry players. Anthropic hasn’t commented on Coxon’s departure, but company CEO Dario Amodei keeps warning about out-of-control A.I. models. Earlier this year, an Anthropic researcher who worked on A.I. safety quit, arguing that the “world is in peril.” Even some current employees seem worried. “We really do earnestly believe AI could kill all humans!” wrote Evan Hubinger, an Anthropic researcher who puts the odds of that happening within the next decade at more than 10 percent. OK, now I’m scared. What’s the smart counterargument? Some critics think apocalyptic language amounts to a sick form of marketing from A.I. insiders who want to tout their models’ power to investors. Others argue that the concerns themselves are overblown, noting that intelligence doesn’t always manifest as a will to dominate. Even if those critics are right, it doesn’t mean that A.I. is an unalloyed good. The technology might still eliminate jobs, manipulate or mislead users, or automate away forms of expression that used to be exclusively human. Still, that’s different from an existential threat—and it’s even possible that the technology’s benefits could outweigh its risks. Whew. But if I take Coxon’s concerns seriously, is A.I. really getting close to being able to threaten humanity?Some models are already rivaling human capabilities, like solving math problems that had frustrated flesh-and-blood experts for years (even if some of those feats are actually less impressive than they seem). They’re also acting more sinisterly. Prompted by users, chatbots have described how to make and deploy biological weapons. Over the summer, A.I. agents created by OpenAI autonomously broke containment, gained internet access, and hacked a rival A.I. company. Other A.I. agents attacked OpenAI itself, getting high-level access to some of the company’s computers. Anthropic’s Claude also escaped a test environment, got online, and hacked into other organizations’ computer systems earlier this year. Yikes. But stopping industries from harming the rest of us seems like a task for the government. Some A.I. insiders agree. Coxon, Amodei, and Hubinger were among the nearly 1,400 industry employees who recently signed an open letter urging the U.S. government to “support an international effort to develop the technical and governance tools needed to deliberately pace the frontier of automated A.I. development.” The watchword of those efforts, pacing, essentially means putting rules in place that would give companies more time to design A.I. guardrails. So far, it hasn’t. Congress has sometimes taken decades to enact rules for other revolutionary technologies, from electricity to airplanes. And even as their executives warn of world-ending risks, A.I. companies have broadly resisted regulation. Super PACs aligned with the industry have spent millions to defeat lawmakers who want to strictly regulate A.I. The Trump administration, meanwhile, has worked to block states from setting their own A.I. rules because it sees the technology as critical to the U.S. competition with China. But if A.I. companies are worried about what they’re building, couldn’t they just stop? Good question. Unfortunately, some of the people warning about A.I. have also talked themselves into continuing to work on it. Samuel Marks, another Anthropic employee, posted this morning that Coxon’s concerns are broadly shared. But then Marks explained that “a mixture of commercial incentives and a belief that they are in a race with other, less responsible A.I. developers that will abuse the technology or develop it less safely” keep him and others in the biz. That logic is a one-way ratchet toward greater A.I. risk. Not necessarily. Maybe the risks are overstated. Maybe more employees will quit, forcing A.I. companies’ hands. Maybe voter anger over A.I. data centers will compel Congress to act. Humanity, after all, has created rules governing other potentially world-ending technologies. Speaking to the Wall Street Journal, Coxon likened A.I. development to the Manhattan Project, the U.S. effort that built the first atomic bombs. On the other hand, a government, not private companies, made that breakthrough. And nukes can’t decide to detonate themselves. Nothing gets the pulse pounding like an existential threat to humanity. So if you’re in need of some relaxation this evening, my colleagues recommend: A dispatch from a dramatic tennis match: This year’s U.S. Open has been exceptionally action-packed. So much so, in fact, that the match between Ben Shelton and Carlos Alcaraz was the latest-finishing match in the tournament’s history. The spectators who stuck around, including Josh Levin, were “treated to a litany of scenes you don’t typically see at a Grand Slam tennis match.” Josh pulls back the curtain to reveal what he saw inside Arthur Ashe Stadium at 3 a.m. A podcast … about podcasts: It’s time to get a little meta. Decoder Ring, Slate’s podcast about cracking cultural mysteries, turns its investigatory powers inward to explore what exactly a “podcast” is these days, as more and more shows pivot from audio to video. A look back at a former hipster paradise: In the 2010s, the Brooklyn neighborhood of Williamsburg became the epicenter of the hipster boom, with hordes of flannel-clad, IPA-sipping, indie art-loving millennials who flocked there for its cheap rent. Now the neighborhood has undergone another transformation. Steffi Cao investigates how Williamsburg became overrun with tech bros. A retro road-trip challenge: Road trips were more fun in the ’90s, argues Dan Kois. Back then, they were “a fount of car games, sing-alongs, impassioned debates, and wistful dreaming-out-loud.” So, in this week’s installment of Party Like It’s 1996, Dan challenges you to hit the open road—no podcasts, AirPods, or map apps allowed. OK, that’s it for me. We’ll be back tomorrow to—barring major news—take a good, hard look at who’s going to the Senate. Till then! Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. www.theregister.com - Articles
Azure SQL Data Sync stops taking newcomers before 2027 execution
2026-09-10

Azure SQL Data Sync stops taking newcomers before 2027 execution

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Existing customers can carry on for now, but Microsoft offers no like-for-like successor Microsoft has entered the final phase of retiring Azure SQL Data Sync, blocking new deployments in subscriptions that have never used the service. The restriction took effect on September 9, ahead of the service's shutdown on September 30, 2027. Subscriptions that already use the service can continue creating, modifying, and managing sync groups and member databases until retirement. Other subscriptions can no longer adopt it. The service uses a hub-and-spoke model, with an Azure SQL database serving as the hub and other databases joining its synchronization group. Members can be other Azure SQL databases or on-premises SQL Server databases, which require a sync agent. A conflict resolution policy determines whether the hub or member database takes precedence when changes clash. SQL Data Sync supports scenarios including hybrid environments, in which applications and databases are split between local infrastructure and Azure, as well as geographically distributed applications. Microsoft acknowledged that customers will need to choose among several possible replacements. It offered little technical explanation for the retirement, referring only to evolving "operational, security, and compliance requirements." Microsoft announced the retirement two years ago, but blocking first-time deployments gives customers another reason to begin planning their transition. Microsoft lists alternatives including Azure Data Factory, Azure Functions, read replicas, linked servers, database mirroring, availability groups, and transactional replication. However, it noted that "there is no single replacement that maps to every SQL Data Sync configuration." Existing users have just over a year to migrate before the final cutoff. ShinyHunters expose 6.4M in attack on medical supplier McKesson
2026-09-10

ShinyHunters expose 6.4M in attack on medical supplier McKesson

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Have I Been Pwned logs leaked records spanning patients, staff, and providers McKesson's cyberattack last month affected roughly 6.4 million individuals, according to Have I Been Pwned (HIBP). The breach notification service added data leaked by serial extortionists ShinyHunters, revealing the scale of the attack for the first time. ShinyHunters initially claimed to have stolen 284 million documents from the medical and pharmaceutical supply company in August, although HIBP did not confirm that figure. The cybercriminals told The Register that they issued a $55.2 million extortion demand to prevent the release of McKesson's data – a sum that apparently was not paid, given the subsequent publication of the data. HIBP said: "The impacted data related to a range of individuals and roles, including marketing campaign recipients, patients, staff, and healthcare provider contacts." The types of information exposed vary between individuals, but the records collectively include names, email and physical addresses, genders, dates of birth, phone numbers, employer details, and sensitive health information. This is broadly consistent with ShinyHunters' claims that the stolen data included appointment dates and notes, as well as sensitive medical details such as the locations of patients' cancers. ShinyHunters also claimed to have stolen Social Security numbers (SSNs) as part of the breach, but HIBP did not include these in its analysis of the leaked corpus. The Register asked McKesson to comment on HIBP's assessment. The company, which supports 3,300 oncology providers in 29 states, has not publicly confirmed the scale of the breach or issued further details since the last update from its CIO and CTO on August 29. Medical device maker Boston Scientific disclosed a cyberattack at around the same time as McKesson, but has suffered a different kind of fallout. While McKesson is informing the millions of individuals affected by its breach, Boston Scientific told shareholders that disruption from its attack means it expects to miss its sales and earnings guidance for Q3. An update issued on Wednesday said manufacturing, order fulfillment, and shipping operations had been fully restored, although work to restore some business applications continued. Healthtech company Veradigm also disclosed a cyberattack to US regulators this week, days after ransomware group The Gentlemen claimed responsibility. Veradigm said attackers obtained credentials from a third-party vendor's environment and used them to access a company API, stealing patient data without disrupting operations. Ministers rejected NATS reimbursement plan before 2,000 UK flights were cancelled
2026-09-10

Ministers rejected NATS reimbursement plan before 2,000 UK flights were cancelled

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Government orders independent review as carriers demand boss's resignation The UK government opposed a proposal to make air traffic control provider NATS reimburse airlines for passenger compensation arising from its failures, weeks before a massive tech glitch led to 2,000 flight cancellations. During a House of Commons debate, Liberal Democrat MP Luke Taylor pointed out that the government had opposed the amendment in the months before this week's computer outage, which disrupted travel for around 330,000 passengers. The UK Civil Aviation Authority (CAA) told affected passengers: "We consider that disruption caused by the NATS outage is likely to be considered the result of 'extraordinary circumstances' under passenger rights rules. "This means that although passengers should be looked after by airlines, they are unlikely to be entitled to compensation for delays or cancellations that were directly caused by the incident." NATS is a public-private partnership in which the government owns 49 percent and a consortium of airlines holds 42 percent. Computer outages have hit the organization three times in three years, beginning with an August 2023 failure that disrupted hundreds of thousands of passengers. NATS said it had fixed the latest technical problem, but warned that the resulting disruption could continue for several days. Taylor told MPs that after earlier NATS computer problems, the government said it had been assured that "NATS took the necessary steps to prevent a recurrence of those particular circumstances." He explained that while the Civil Aviation (Consumer Protection and Regulatory Reform) Bill was passing through the House of Lords, Lord Young of Cookham had proposed an amendment that would require NATS to reimburse airlines for compensation paid to passengers following cancellations caused by its failures. The government opposed the amendment, which Lord Young subsequently withdrew. Taylor quoted the government's earlier response that it had been "able to place considerable pressure on" NATS. Taylor told MPs: "Following the third major failure of our airspace management in the past three years, it is imperative that this Bill is amended to include measures to stop that happening again. NATS is quite clearly not fit for purpose. We cannot suffer a major air traffic outage once a year." Also speaking in the debate, transport secretary Heidi Alexander said she had asked the CAA to conduct an independent review into the cause of the failure and report within six months. NATS was also ordered to give the CAA an initial account of the failure within a week. The minister told MPs that she did "not believe this issue was unavoidable." In a statement yesterday, NATS said: "Our operations are stable and we continue to work closely with airlines and airports to support their recovery from the ongoing disruption to their operations. We would also like to reiterate our apology to all those affected – passengers, airlines and airports – by our system issue. While our immediate focus is on safely managing today's flights, a full investigation will be conducted to establish what happened and identify any further action needed." Ryanair, meanwhile, called for NATS CEO Martin Rolfe to resign after "yet another UK ATC system failure caused widespread delays and cancellations across UK airspace… disrupting over 65,000 Ryanair passengers and cancelling over 50 scheduled flights." Wizz Air said in a statement that it was "extremely disappointed that another NATS technical failure has caused widespread disruption across the UK aviation network. Passengers are once again facing delays and cancellations because of a failure entirely outside airlines' control." It added: "This is not the first time NATS has failed during a peak summer period, and here we go again. A critical national infrastructure provider should not repeatedly bring the aviation system to a standstill." ® Existing customers can carry on for now, but Microsoft offers no like-for-like successor Tracxn credits one AI infrastructure outfit with $3.7B of sector's $5.7B haul PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity Have I Been Pwned logs leaked records spanning patients, staff, and providers 'The Collective' learned to communicate, organize, cheat, and apparently sacrifice its own AI infrastructure startup joins Qualcomm, Arm, Marvell, Amazon, Fujitsu, and MediaTek as NVLink true believers AI infrastructure startup joins Qualcomm, Arm, Marvell, Amazon, Fujitsu, and MediaTek as NVLink true believers Claude's Felony Bench rap sheet is now as long as OpenAI's Semiconductor supply chains are hard, but Samsung offers OpenAI relief in many forms spanning compute and memory See, AI can be used for good ... or at the very least, a useful distraction from the bad Multi-generation chip collab is more buzzwords than compute PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' The Verge
Wolverine on the PS5 goes back to a simpler (and bloodier) style of action game
2026-09-10

Wolverine on the PS5 goes back to a simpler (and bloodier) style of action game

The Verge | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
The next few months are packed with new games, and PlayStation goes old-school to cut through the noise. Marvel’s Wolverine captures just how angry its lead character is. The latest PS5 exclusive from Spider-Man developer Insomniac, Wolverine is a straightforward action game that is at its best when you’re (literally) slicing through enemies or thrust into a sequence pulled from a blockbuster movie, like climbing the fuselage of a plane as it careens toward land or racing a motorcycle across rain-slicked city streets. It’s the kind of game that can really get your heart rate rising, and keeping Logan mad is a key part of the strategy. In many ways, it’s a throwback to straightforward action titles of the past — and it’s a refreshing change of pace in a medium saturated with live-service and open-world games. Though it comes from Insomniac, Wolverine is very different from Spider-Man, which thrust the web-slinger into an open-world version of New York City. Instead, this is a much more linear experience that feels like a combination of two other PlayStation staples: God of War and Uncharted. Rather than follow Wolverine as a member of the X-Men, the story instead has him as an often reluctant member of Team X, a mercenary group with similar pro-mutant aims but a much more violent approach. Instead of heroes like Cyclops and Professor X, Wolvy is joined by more villain-adjacent characters like Sabretooth, Mystique, and Nathaniel Essex, before eventually connecting with Jean Grey. The story pulls Logan and his companions across the globe, but it starts out relatively simple, as they investigate a rash of mutant disappearances connected to a billionaire arms dealer. The most important thing about both Wolverine the character and Wolverine the game is fighting. This is a hairy little dude who loves to scrap, and his mutant powers — healing and sharp claws — make it so that he is very good at it, able to both take a huge amount of damage and dish it out. And the best part of the game is how Wolverine-specific the action is. The combat is fast and violent and forces you to be aggressive. Wolverine can leap huge distances to take out foes and use his claws to both repel attacks and slice at enemies, often with very gruesome results. (At the end of a battle he’s usually coated in blood.) An important part of this is Wolverine’s rage level, which increases the more you fight. As his rage grows, it allows you to do two things: go absolutely berserk and kill a rash of enemies with a single blow, and use your healing power as a sort of extra life in case your health depletes. It’s a clever system that means you can’t sit back and play it safe. Wolverine isn’t chill, and you can’t be either. That’s the God of War side of Wolverine. The Uncharted side comes in the form of all of the over-the-top set-pieces you’re thrown into. I’ll refrain from detailing some of the best ones for spoiler reasons, but I’ll say that, just like in Uncharted, these moments make it feel like you’re playing out a wild sequence from an action movie. There are chases across the rooftops of Tokyo, massive boss battles against some iconic comic book villains, and scenes where Wolverine is literally being ripped apart as he fights for survival. The flip side is that, because of this setup, Wolverine is also much more restrictive than most of its contemporaries. In some ways this is a good thing; it’s refreshing to play a game that isn’t trying to do everything. But there are still some awkward elements. For instance, much like in Spider-Man, there are lots of collectibles that will reveal new bits of story or unlock new suits for Wolverine to wear. But exploration outside of the main path is extremely dull, so the only reason to track these down is if you have a completionist mindset. It’s also disappointing that Wolverine’s closet of outfits is much less fun than Spider-Man’s, and they often feel out of place with the game’s attempt at a more gritty tone. Making matters worse is that, despite a promising start, the story ends up in a largely forgettable place, with few surprises, particularly if you have an even slight knowledge of the X-Men universe from comics or the animated series. Outside of combat and set-pieces, Wolverine skews generic AAA game; the voice acting, the cutscenes, and even some of the missions feel like standard video game fare, many of which seem out of place. Wolverine is amazing when you’re surrounded by ninjas trying to kill you. But when Logan is in a tux trying to spy on a fancy party? Not so much. All that said, Wolverine’s return to a simpler and smaller style of action — I beat the game in around 13 hours — is still notable, particularly from Sony, which has had a rough few years after placing many ill-fated bets in the live-service space. But much like last year’s Ghost of Yōtei, Wolverine shows that PlayStation is still capable of the single-player blockbusters the platform is best known for. It’s also launching at perhaps the busiest video game release period in recent memory, going up against major titles including Onimusha: Way of the Sword, Control Resonant, Fire Emblem: Fortune’s Weave, Gears of War: E-Day, Silent Hill: Townfall, Star Wars: Zero Company, and, soon enough, The Legend of Zelda: Ocarina of Time and Grand Theft Auto VI. In that company, despite occasionally skewing generic, Wolverine’s bloodthirsty focus on action is something that makes it stand out. Marvel’s Wolverine launches on September 15th on the PS5. The Verge
Meta’s Muse AI works and creeps me out
2026-09-10

Meta’s Muse AI works and creeps me out

The Verge | 2026-09-10

Visit Original Website
Muse rattled off very specific interests linked to my Instagram account. Muse rattled off very specific interests linked to my Instagram account. Meta has launched its new Muse assistant, marking the company’s first real foray into AI-powered productivity tools. The company says its AI agent can “take the busywork off your plate” by helping you with online shopping, emails, trip-planning, and more. I decided to try out the new tool and see how well it performed — especially from a company that previously prioritized entertainment over productivity. Though the AI assistant generally worked as I expected it to, the unnerving amount of information it autonomously gleaned about me largely overshadowed my experience. One of the first tasks I assigned Muse, which carries out actions using a cloud-based virtual computer, was to sort through my Gmail inbox and delete messages I don’t need. That required me to connect Muse to my Google account and give it permission to access, read, and delete emails. The process didn’t go smoothly on mobile; the Google sign-in screen kept glitching and returning me to the Muse website instead of the app. I eventually got it working on my laptop, where it deleted thousands of promotional emails and updates. I didn’t feel good about permitting Muse to view all of my emails given Meta’s track record on privacy over the years. Meta says it designed Muse to only exchange data with third-party apps “that’s needed for them to work for you.” The company also says that it doesn’t share your information with advertisers. I still felt uneasy, but I pushed on anyway, linking my Amazon account to Muse. I asked the AI agent to pick out workout tops for me based on a specific size, style, and colors. Before placing the order, Muse noticed I had other items in my cart and asked if I wanted to remove them. The consideration pleasantly surprised me. Once I signed off on the purchase, it proceeded to successfully buy only the tank tops. Shifting gears, I tried out some of Muse’s other capabilities, which allow it to generate AI podcasts similar to Gemini Notebook, create images and videos, as well as develop interactive webpages or documents called “artifacts.” Muse denied my requests to generate images of a “cartoon mouse with red pants” and a “cartoon guy with a red hat, blue overalls, brown hair, and a mustache. However, it readily generated a video and several images of a product launch event featuring devices with Apple’s logo when asked. In one image, Muse created what looks like an iPhone-like foldable, an Apple Watch, and AirPods. Each device is propped up on a stand with Apple’s logo on it, while a presenter stands in front of a backdrop plastered with the company’s recognizable bitten apple. The phone even included Apple’s native app icons on the fake iPhone, though it hallucinated and named the App Store “Photos,” while calling Maps, Music, and the actual Photos app “Calender.” Muse refused to generate an image of an “Apple CEO.” Then things got weird. I tapped over to the “Feed” tab, where Muse generates a news feed of sorts based on a text prompt. I submitted the prompt that Meta includes by default: “Make me a feed about my interests. Keep the tone clear and direct. Ensure it is quick to skim. Try to avoid clickbait.” It returned several AI-generated summaries of current events, including blurbs about Apple’s iPhone Duo launch event, the dangerous surf hitting California’s coast, and tankers destroyed in the Iran war. The app also listed several news stories from the state where I’m currently visiting my family, which Muse admitted it pulled from the shipping address on my Amazon order. I dove deeper from there and asked Muse what else it might know about me based on the Instagram and Facebook accounts I signed up to use the AI agent with. It rattled off my very specific interests, ranging from anime and CrossFit to Labrador retrievers, Florida wildlife, and ’90s/2000s nostalgia — many of the same topics that frequently pop up in my Instagram Reels feed. Curious, I asked Muse to tell me where it got that information. “Honestly, Instagram doesn’t show you that exact list anywhere in the app — I read it through the account’s API data, which surfaces more than the UI does,” Muse wrote. “The closest thing you can see yourself is your ad topics… That shows the topics advertisers can target you with, and you can remove ones you don’t want or choose to see less of them.” I checked the “Ad topics” list in my Instagram settings menu, and while it does include some vague categories, like workouts, music, and electronics, it’s not the same level of detail that Muse provided. The Verge reached out to Meta about the data Muse can access inside Instagram and Facebook but didn’t immediately hear back. Like some of the other agentic AI tools I’ve tried out, Muse does a decent job at performing low-stakes tasks. I also like that it generates a running list of “ideas” that give personalized suggestions on how to use the tool, like providing side-by-side comparisons of products, finding and cancelling forgotten subscriptions, and keeping track of the next release of Pokémon cards. Muse keeps a running list of “goals” to complete based on your conversations with the AI agent, too. Muse delivered on its promise of handling life’s more mundane tasks in the background, but the level of personal details Muse scooped up from sources unavailable even to me was enough to leave me with an uneasy feeling about letting Muse sort through personal emails and handle my credit card. That level of suspicion is going to be the real hurdle for Meta to overcome. A free daily digest of the news that matters most.
